[Narrator] The Hunter hunted The Epilogue CHAPTER I. THE STRANGE MAN ’S ARRIVAL The stranger came early in February, one wintry day, through a biting wind and a driving snow, the last snowfall of the year, over the down, walking from Bramblehurst railway station, and carrying a little black portmanteau in his thickly gloved hand. [/]
[Narrator] He was wrapped up from head to foot, and the brim of his soft felt hat hid every inch of his face but the shiny tip of his nose; the snow had piled itself against his shoulders and chest, and added a white crest to the burden he carried. [/]
[MrMarvel] He staggered into the “ Coach and Horses ” more dead than alive, and flung his portmanteau down. [/]
[MrMarvel] “ A fire, ” he cried, “ in the name of human charity! [/]
[MrMarvel] A room and a fire! ” [/]
[Narrator] He stamped and shook the snow from off himself in the bar, and followed Mrs. Hall into her guest parlour to strike his bargain. [/]
[Narrator] And with that much introduction, that and a couple of sovereigns flung upon the table, he took up his quarters in the inn. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Hall lit the fire and left him there while she went to prepare him a meal with her own hands. [/]
[MrsHall] A guest to stop at Iping in the wintertime was an unheard - of piece of luck, let alone a guest who was no “ haggler, ” and she was resolved to show herself worthy of her good fortune. [/]
[Narrator] As soon as the bacon was well under way, and Millie, her lymphatic maid, had been brisked up a bit by a few deftly chosen expressions of contempt, she carried the cloth, plates, and glasses into the parlour and began to lay them with the utmost _ éclat _. [/]
[Narrator] Although the fire was burning up briskly, she was surprised to see that her visitor still wore his hat and coat, standing with his back to her and staring out of the window at the falling snow in the yard. [/]
[Narrator] His gloved hands were clasped behind him, and he seemed to be lost in thought. [/]
[Narrator] She noticed that the melting snow that still sprinkled his shoulders dripped upon her carpet. [/]
[Narrator] “ Can I take your hat and coat, sir? ” she said, “ and give them a good dry in the kitchen? ” “ No, ” he said without turning. [/]
[Narrator] She was not sure she had heard him, and was about to repeat her question. [/]
[Narrator] He turned his head and looked at her over his shoulder. [/]
[MrMarvel] “ I prefer to keep them on, ” he said with emphasis, and she noticed that he wore big blue spectacles with sidelights, and had a bush side - whisker over his coat - collar that completely hid his cheeks and face. [/]
[Millie] “ Very well, sir, ” she said. [/]
[Millie] “ _ As _ you like. [/]
[Millie] In a bit the room will be warmer. ” [/]
[Narrator] He made no answer, and had turned his face away from her again, and Mrs. Hall, feeling that her conversational advances were ill - timed, laid the rest of the table things in a quick staccato and whisked out of the room. [/]
[Narrator] When she returned he was still standing there, like a man of stone, his back hunched, his collar turned up, his dripping hat - brim turned down, hiding his face and ears completely. [/]
[Millie] She put down the eggs and bacon with considerable emphasis, and called rather than said to him, “ Your lunch is served, sir. ” [/]
[MrMarvel] “ Thank you, ” he said at the same time, and did not stir until she was closing the door. [/]
[Narrator] Then he swung round and approached the table with a certain eager quickness. [/]
[Narrator] As she went behind the bar to the kitchen she heard a sound repeated at regular intervals. [/]
[Narrator] Chirk, chirk, chirk, it went, the sound of a spoon being rapidly whisked round a basin. [/]
[Millie] “ That girl! ” [/]
[Narrator] she said. [/]
[Millie] “ There! [/]
[Millie] I clean forgot it. [/]
[Millie] It ’s her being so long! ” [/]
[Narrator] And while she herself finished mixing the mustard, she gave Millie a few verbal stabs for her excessive slowness. [/]
[Narrator] She had cooked the ham and eggs, laid the table, and done everything, while Millie (help indeed!) had only succeeded in delaying the mustard. [/]
[Narrator] And him a new guest and wanting to stay! [/]
[Narrator] Then she filled the mustard pot, and, putting it with a certain stateliness upon a gold and black tea - tray, carried it into the parlour. [/]
[Narrator] She rapped and entered promptly. [/]
[Narrator] As she did so her visitor moved quickly, so that she got but a glimpse of a white object disappearing behind the table. [/]
[Narrator] It would seem he was picking something from the floor. [/]
[Narrator] She rapped down the mustard pot on the table, and then she noticed the overcoat and hat had been taken off and put over a chair in front of the fire, and a pair of wet boots threatened rust to her steel fender. [/]
[Narrator] She went to these things resolutely. [/]
[Millie] “ I suppose I may have them to dry now, ” she said in a voice that brooked no denial. [/]
[HerVisitor] “ Leave the hat, ” said her visitor, in a muffled voice, and turning she saw he had raised his head and was sitting and looking at her. [/]
[Narrator] For a moment she stood gaping at him, too surprised to speak. [/]
[Narrator] He held a white cloth — it was a serviette he had brought with him — over the lower part of his face, so that his mouth and jaws were completely hidden, and that was the reason of his muffled voice. [/]
[Narrator] But it was not that which startled Mrs. Hall. [/]
[Narrator] It was the fact that all his forehead above his blue glasses was covered by a white bandage, and that another covered his ears, leaving not a scrap of his face exposed excepting only his pink, peaked nose. [/]
[Narrator] It was bright, pink, and shiny just as it had been at first. [/]
[Narrator] He wore a dark - brown velvet jacket with a high, black, linen - lined collar turned up about his neck. [/]
[Narrator] The thick black hair, escaping as it could below and between the cross bandages, projected in curious tails and horns, giving him the strangest appearance conceivable. [/]
[Narrator] This muffled and bandaged head was so unlike what she had anticipated, that for a moment she was rigid. [/]
[Narrator] He did not remove the serviette, but remained holding it, as she saw now, with a brown gloved hand, and regarding her with his inscrutable blue glasses. [/]
[HerVisitor] “ Leave the hat, ” he said, speaking very distinctly through the white cloth. [/]
[Narrator] Her nerves began to recover from the shock they had received. [/]
[Narrator] She placed the hat on the chair again by the fire. [/]
[Millie] “ I did n’t know, sir, ” she began, “ that — ” and she stopped embarrassed. [/]
[HerVisitor] “ Thank you, ” he said drily, glancing from her to the door and then at her again. [/]
[Millie] “ I ’ll have them nicely dried, sir, at once, ” she said, and carried his clothes out of the room. [/]
[Narrator] She glanced at his white - swathed head and blue goggles again as she was going out of the door; but his napkin was still in front of his face. [/]
[Narrator] She shivered a little as she closed the door behind her, and her face was eloquent of her surprise and perplexity. [/]
[Millie] “ I _ never _, ” she whispered. [/]
[Millie] “ There! ” [/]
[Narrator] She went quite softly to the kitchen, and was too preoccupied to ask Millie what she was messing about with _ now _, when she got there. [/]
[Narrator] The visitor sat and listened to her retreating feet. [/]
[Narrator] He glanced inquiringly at the window before he removed his serviette, and resumed his meal. [/]
[Narrator] He took a mouthful, glanced suspiciously at the window, took another mouthful, then rose and, taking the serviette in his hand, walked across the room and pulled the blind down to the top of the white muslin that obscured the lower panes. [/]
[Narrator] This left the room in a twilight. [/]
[Narrator] This done, he returned with an easier air to the table and his meal. [/]
[MrsHall] “ The poor soul ’s had an accident or an op’ration or somethin’, ” said Mrs. Hall. [/]
[MrsHall] “ What a turn them bandages did give me, to be sure! ” [/]
[Narrator] She put on some more coal, unfolded the clothes - horse, and extended the traveller ’s coat upon this. [/]
[MrsHall] “ And they goggles! [/]
[MrsHall] Why, he looked more like a divin ’ helmet than a human man! ” [/]
[Narrator] She hung his muffler on a corner of the horse. [/]
[MrsHall] “ And holding that handkerchief over his mouth all the time. Talkin ’ through it! .. . [/]
[MrsHall] Perhaps his mouth was hurt too — maybe. ” [/]
[Narrator] She turned round, as one who suddenly remembers. [/]
[MrsHall] “ Bless my soul alive! ” she said, going off at a tangent; “ ai n’t you done them taters _ yet _, Millie? ” [/]
[Narrator] When Mrs. Hall went to clear away the stranger ’s lunch, her idea that his mouth must also have been cut or disfigured in the accident she supposed him to have suffered, was confirmed, for he was smoking a pipe, and all the time that she was in the room he never loosened the silk muffler he had wrapped round the lower part of his face to put the mouthpiece to his lips. [/]
[Narrator] Yet it was not forgetfulness, for she saw he glanced at it as it smouldered out. [/]
[Narrator] He sat in the corner with his back to the window - blind and spoke now, having eaten and drunk and being comfortably warmed through, with less aggressive brevity than before. [/]
[Narrator] The reflection of the fire lent a kind of red animation to his big spectacles they had lacked hitherto. [/]
[HerVisitor] “ I have some luggage, ” he said, “ at Bramblehurst station, ” and he asked her how he could have it sent. [/]
[Narrator] He bowed his bandaged head quite politely in acknowledgment of her explanation. [/]
[HerVisitor] “ To - morrow? ” he said. [/]
[Narrator] “ There is no speedier delivery? ” and seemed quite disappointed when she answered, “ No. ” [/]
[Narrator] Was she quite sure? [/]
[Narrator] No man with a trap who would go over? Mrs. Hall, nothing loath, answered his questions and developed a conversation. [/]
[MrsHall] “ It ’s a steep road by the down, sir, ” she said in answer to the question about a trap; and then, snatching at an opening, said, “ It was there a carriage was upsettled, a year ago and more. [/]
[MrsHall] A gentleman killed, besides his coachman. [/]
[MrsHall] Accidents, sir, happen in a moment, do n’t they? ” [/]
[Narrator] But the visitor was not to be drawn so easily. [/]
[HerVisitor] “ They do, ” he said through his muffler, eyeing her quietly through his impenetrable glasses. [/]
[HerVisitor] “ But they take long enough to get well, do n’t they? [/]
[HerVisitor] . .. [/]
[HerVisitor] There was my sister ’s son, Tom, jest cut his arm with a scythe, tumbled on it in the ’ ayfield, and, bless me! [/]
[HerVisitor] he was three months tied up sir. [/]
[HerVisitor] You ’d hardly believe it. [/]
[Narrator] It ’s regular given me a dread of a scythe, sir. ” “ I can quite understand that, ” said the visitor. [/]
[TheVisitor] “ He was afraid, one time, that he ’d have to have an op’ration — he was that bad, sir. ” [/]
[Narrator] The visitor laughed abruptly, a bark of a laugh that he seemed to bite and kill in his mouth. [/]
[TheVisitor] “ _ Was _ he? ” he said. [/]
[TheVisitor] “ He was, sir. [/]
[TheVisitor] And no laughing matter to them as had the doing for him, as I had — my sister being took up with her little ones so much. [/]
[TheVisitor] There was bandages to do, sir, and bandages to undo. [/]
[TheVisitor] So that if I may make so bold as to say it, sir — ” “ Will you get me some matches? ” said the visitor, quite abruptly. [/]
[TheVisitor] “ My pipe is out. ” [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Hall was pulled up suddenly. [/]
[Narrator] It was certainly rude of him, after telling him all she had done. [/]
[Narrator] She gasped at him for a moment, and remembered the two sovereigns. [/]
[Narrator] She went for the matches. [/]
[TheVisitor] “ Thanks, ” he said concisely, as she put them down, and turned his shoulder upon her and stared out of the window again. [/]
[Narrator] It was altogether too discouraging. [/]
[Narrator] Evidently he was sensitive on the topic of operations and bandages. [/]
[MrsHall] She did not “ make so bold as to say, ” however, after all. [/]
[Narrator] But his snubbing way had irritated her, and Millie had a hot time of it that afternoon. [/]
[Narrator] The visitor remained in the parlour until four o’clock, without giving the ghost of an excuse for an intrusion. [/]
[Narrator] For the most part he was quite still during that time; it would seem he sat in the growing darkness smoking in the firelight — perhaps dozing. [/]
[Narrator] Once or twice a curious listener might have heard him at the coals, and for the space of five minutes he was audible pacing the room. [/]
[Narrator] He seemed to be talking to himself. [/]
[Narrator] Then the armchair creaked as he sat down again. CHAPTER II. MR. TEDDY HENFREY ’S FIRST IMPRESSIONS At four o’clock, when it was fairly dark and Mrs. Hall was screwing up her courage to go in and ask her visitor if he would take some tea, Teddy Henfrey, the clock - jobber, came into the bar. [/]
[Henfrey] “ My sakes! [/]
[Henfrey] Mrs. Hall, ” said he, “ but this is terrible weather for thin boots! ” [/]
[Narrator] The snow outside was falling faster. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Hall agreed, and then noticed he had his bag with him. [/]
[MrsHall] “ Now you ’re here, Mr. Teddy, ” said she, “ I ’d be glad if you ’d give th ’ old clock in the parlour a bit of a look. [/]
[MrsHall] ’ Tis going, and it strikes well and hearty; but the hour - hand wo n’t do nuthin’ but point at six. ” And leading the way, she went across to the parlour door and rapped and entered. [/]
[Narrator] Her visitor, she saw as she opened the door, was seated in the armchair before the fire, dozing it would seem, with his bandaged head drooping on one side. [/]
[Narrator] The only light in the room was the red glow from the fire — which lit his eyes like adverse railway signals, but left his downcast face in darkness — and the scanty vestiges of the day that came in through the open door. [/]
[Narrator] Everything was ruddy, shadowy, and indistinct to her, the more so since she had just been lighting the bar lamp, and her eyes were dazzled. [/]
[Narrator] But for a second it seemed to her that the man she looked at had an enormous mouth wide open — a vast and incredible mouth that swallowed the whole of the lower portion of his face. [/]
[Narrator] It was the sensation of a moment: the white - bound head, the monstrous goggle eyes, and this huge yawn below it. [/]
[Narrator] Then he stirred, started up in his chair, put up his hand. [/]
[Narrator] She opened the door wide, so that the room was lighter, and she saw him more clearly, with the muffler held up to his face just as she had seen him hold the serviette before. [/]
[Narrator] The shadows, she fancied, had tricked her. [/]
[MrsHall] “ Would you mind, sir, this man a - coming to look at the clock, sir? ” [/]
[Narrator] she said, recovering from the momentary shock. [/]
[Henfrey] “ Look at the clock? ” he said, staring round in a drowsy manner, and speaking over his hand, and then, getting more fully awake, “ certainly. ” [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Hall went away to get a lamp, and he rose and stretched himself. [/]
[Narrator] Then came the light, and Mr. Teddy Henfrey, entering, was confronted by this bandaged person. [/]
[Narrator] He was, he says, “ taken aback. ” “ Good afternoon, ” said the stranger, regarding him — as Mr. Henfrey says, with a vivid sense of the dark spectacles—“like a lobster. ” [/]
[Henfrey] “ I hope, ” said Mr. Henfrey, “ that it ’s no intrusion. ” [/]
[TheStranger] “ None whatever, ” said the stranger. [/]
[Narrator] “ Though, I understand, ” he said turning to Mrs. Hall, “ that this room is really to be mine for my own private use. ” “ I thought, sir, ” said Mrs. Hall, “ you ’d prefer the clock — ” “ Certainly, ” said the stranger, “ certainly — but, as a rule, I like to be alone and undisturbed. [/]
[Henfrey] “ But I ’m really glad to have the clock seen to, ” he said, seeing a certain hesitation in Mr. Henfrey ’s manner. [/]
[Henfrey] “ Very glad. ” [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Henfrey had intended to apologise and withdraw, but this anticipation reassured him. [/]
[Narrator] The stranger turned round with his back to the fireplace and put his hands behind his back. [/]
[TheStranger] “ And presently, ” he said, “ when the clock - mending is over, I think I should like to have some tea. [/]
[TheStranger] But not till the clock - mending is over. ” [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Hall was about to leave the room — she made no conversational advances this time, because she did not want to be snubbed in front of Mr. Henfrey — when her visitor asked her if she had made any arrangements about his boxes at Bramblehurst. [/]
[Narrator] She told him she had mentioned the matter to the postman, and that the carrier could bring them over on the morrow. [/]
[HerVisitor] “ You are certain that is the earliest? ” he said. [/]
[Narrator] She was certain, with a marked coldness. [/]
[Narrator] “ I should explain, ” he added, “ what I was really too cold and fatigued to do before, that I am an experimental investigator. ” “ Indeed, sir, ” said Mrs. Hall, much impressed. [/]
[MrsHall] “ And my baggage contains apparatus and appliances. ” “ Very useful things indeed they are, sir, ” said Mrs. Hall. [/]
[MrsHall] “ And I ’m very naturally anxious to get on with my inquiries. ” “ Of course, sir. ” [/]
[HerVisitor] “ My reason for coming to Iping, ” he proceeded, with a certain deliberation of manner, “ was. .. a desire for solitude. [/]
[HerVisitor] I do not wish to be disturbed in my work. [/]
[Narrator] In addition to my work, an accident — ” “ I thought as much, ” said Mrs. Hall to herself. [/]
[HerVisitor] “ — necessitates a certain retirement. [/]
[HerVisitor] My eyes — are sometimes so weak and painful that I have to shut myself up in the dark for hours together. [/]
[HerVisitor] Lock myself up. [/]
[HerVisitor] Sometimes — now and then. Not at present, certainly. [/]
[HerVisitor] At such times the slightest disturbance, the entry of a stranger into the room, is a source of excruciating annoyance to me — it is well these things should be understood. ” [/]
[MrsHall] “ Certainly, sir, ” said Mrs. Hall. [/]
[Narrator] “ And if I might make so bold as to ask — ” “ That I think, is all, ” said the stranger, with that quietly irresistible air of finality he could assume at will. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Hall reserved her question and sympathy for a better occasion. [/]
[Narrator] After Mrs. Hall had left the room, he remained standing in front of the fire, glaring, so Mr. Henfrey puts it, at the clock - mending. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Henfrey not only took off the hands of the clock, and the face, but extracted the works; and he tried to work in as slow and quiet and unassuming a manner as possible. [/]
[Narrator] He worked with the lamp close to him, and the green shade threw a brilliant light upon his hands, and upon the frame and wheels, and left the rest of the room shadowy. [/]
[Narrator] When he looked up, coloured patches swam in his eyes. [/]
[Narrator] Being constitutionally of a curious nature, he had removed the works — a quite unnecessary proceeding — with the idea of delaying his departure and perhaps falling into conversation with the stranger. [/]
[Narrator] But the stranger stood there, perfectly silent and still. [/]
[Narrator] So still, it got on Henfrey ’s nerves. [/]
[Narrator] He felt alone in the room and looked up, and there, grey and dim, was the bandaged head and huge blue lenses staring fixedly, with a mist of green spots drifting in front of them. [/]
[Narrator] It was so uncanny to Henfrey that for a minute they remained staring blankly at one another. [/]
[Narrator] Then Henfrey looked down again. [/]
[Narrator] Very uncomfortable position! [/]
[Narrator] One would like to say something. [/]
[Narrator] Should he remark that the weather was very cold for the time of year? [/]
[Narrator] He looked up as if to take aim with that introductory shot. [/]
[Henfrey] “ The weather — ” he began. [/]
[TheRigidFigure] “ Why do n’t you finish and go? ” said the rigid figure, evidently in a state of painfully suppressed rage. [/]
[TheRigidFigure] “ All you ’ve got to do is to fix the hour - hand on its axle. [/]
[Narrator] You ’re simply humbugging — ” “ Certainly, sir — one minute more. [/]
[Henfrey] I overlooked — ” and Mr. Henfrey finished and went. [/]
[Narrator] But he went feeling excessively annoyed. [/]
[Henfrey] “ Damn it! ” said Mr. Henfrey to himself, trudging down the village through the thawing snow; “ a man must do a clock at times, surely. ” [/]
[MrsHall] And again, “ Ca n’t a man look at you? —Ugly! ” [/]
[MrsHall] And yet again, “ Seemingly not. [/]
[MrsHall] If the police was wanting you you could n’t be more wropped and bandaged. ” [/]
[MrsHall] At Gleeson ’s corner he saw Hall, who had recently married the stranger ’s hostess at the “ Coach and Horses, ” and who now drove the Iping conveyance, when occasional people required it, to Sidderbridge Junction, coming towards him on his return from that place. [/]
[MrsHall] Hall had evidently been “ stopping a bit ” at Sidderbridge, to judge by his driving. [/]
[Henfrey] “ ’ Ow do, Teddy? ” he said, passing. [/]
[Henfrey] “ You got a rum un up home! ” said Teddy. [/]
[Narrator] Hall very sociably pulled up. [/]
[Henfrey] “ What ’s that? ” he asked. [/]
[Henfrey] “ Rum - looking customer stopping at the ‘ Coach and Horses, ’ ” said Teddy. [/]
[Henfrey] “ My sakes! ” [/]
[Narrator] And he proceeded to give Hall a vivid description of his grotesque guest. [/]
[Henfrey] “ Looks a bit like a disguise, do n’t it? [/]
[Henfrey] I ’d like to see a man ’s face if I had him stopping in _ my _ place, ” said Henfrey. [/]
[Henfrey] “ But women are that trustful — where strangers are concerned. [/]
[Narrator] He ’s took your rooms and he ai n’t even given a name, Hall. ” “ You do n’t say so! ” said Hall, who was a man of sluggish apprehension. [/]
[Henfrey] “ Yes, ” said Teddy. [/]
[Henfrey] “ By the week. [/]
[Henfrey] Whatever he is, you ca n’t get rid of him under the week. [/]
[Henfrey] And he ’s got a lot of luggage coming to - morrow, so he says. [/]
[Henfrey] Let ’s hope it wo n’t be stones in boxes, Hall. ” He told Hall how his aunt at Hastings had been swindled by a stranger with empty portmanteaux. [/]
[Narrator] Altogether he left Hall vaguely suspicious. [/]
[MrsHall] “ Get up, old girl, ” said Hall. [/]
[MrsHall] “ I s’pose I must see ’bout this. ” [/]
[Narrator] Teddy trudged on his way with his mind considerably relieved. [/]
[Henfrey] Instead of “ seeing ’bout it, ” however, Hall on his return was severely rated by his wife on the length of time he had spent in Sidderbridge, and his mild inquiries were answered snappishly and in a manner not to the point. [/]
[Narrator] But the seed of suspicion Teddy had sown germinated in the mind of Mr. Hall in spite of these discouragements. [/]
[MrHall] “ You wim ’ do n’t know everything, ” said Mr. Hall, resolved to ascertain more about the personality of his guest at the earliest possible opportunity. [/]
[Narrator] And after the stranger had gone to bed, which he did about half - past nine, Mr. Hall went very aggressively into the parlour and looked very hard at his wife ’s furniture, just to show that the stranger was n’t master there, and scrutinised closely and a little contemptuously a sheet of mathematical computations the stranger had left. [/]
[Narrator] When retiring for the night he instructed Mrs. Hall to look very closely at the stranger ’s luggage when it came next day. [/]
[MrsHall] “ You mind your own business, Hall, ” said Mrs. Hall, “ and I ’ll mind mine. ” [/]
[Narrator] She was all the more inclined to snap at Hall because the stranger was undoubtedly an unusually strange sort of stranger, and she was by no means assured about him in her own mind. [/]
[Narrator] In the middle of the night she woke up dreaming of huge white heads like turnips, that came trailing after her, at the end of interminable necks, and with vast black eyes. [/]
[Narrator] But being a sensible woman, she subdued her terrors and turned over and went to sleep again. [/]
[Narrator] CHAPTER III. [/]
[Narrator] THE THOUSAND AND ONE BOTTLES [/]
[Narrator] So it was that on the twenty - ninth day of February, at the beginning of the thaw, this singular person fell out of infinity into Iping village. [/]
[Narrator] Next day his luggage arrived through the slush — and very remarkable luggage it was. [/]
[Narrator] There were a couple of trunks indeed, such as a rational man might need, but in addition there were a box of books — big, fat books, of which some were just in an incomprehensible handwriting — and a dozen or more crates, boxes, and cases, containing objects packed in straw, as it seemed to Hall, tugging with a casual curiosity at the straw — glass bottles. [/]
[Narrator] The stranger, muffled in hat, coat, gloves, and wrapper, came out impatiently to meet Fearenside ’s cart, while Hall was having a word or so of gossip preparatory to helping bring them in. [/]
[Narrator] Out he came, not noticing Fearenside ’s dog, who was sniffing in a _ dilettante _ spirit at Hall ’s legs. [/]
[TheStranger] “ Come along with those boxes, ” he said. [/]
[TheStranger] “ I ’ve been waiting long enough. ” [/]
[Narrator] And he came down the steps towards the tail of the cart as if to lay hands on the smaller crate. [/]
[Narrator] No sooner had Fearenside ’s dog caught sight of him, however, than it began to bristle and growl savagely, and when he rushed down the steps it gave an undecided hop, and then sprang straight at his hand. [/]
[Narrator] “ Whup! ” cried Hall, jumping back, for he was no hero with dogs, and Fearenside howled, “ Lie down! ” and snatched his whip. [/]
[Narrator] They saw the dog ’s teeth had slipped the hand, heard a kick, saw the dog execute a flanking jump and get home on the stranger ’s leg, and heard the rip of his trousering. [/]
[Narrator] Then the finer end of Fearenside ’s whip reached his property, and the dog, yelping with dismay, retreated under the wheels of the waggon. [/]
[Narrator] It was all the business of a swift half - minute. [/]
[Narrator] No one spoke, everyone shouted. [/]
[Narrator] The stranger glanced swiftly at his torn glove and at his leg, made as if he would stoop to the latter, then turned and rushed swiftly up the steps into the inn. [/]
[Narrator] They heard him go headlong across the passage and up the uncarpeted stairs to his bedroom. [/]
[Fearenside] “ You brute, you! ” said Fearenside, climbing off the waggon with his whip in his hand, while the dog watched him through the wheel. [/]
[Fearensideyoud] “ Come here, ” said Fearenside—“You’d better. ” [/]

[MrsHall] “ He wuz bit, ” said Hall. [/]
[TheStranger] “ I ’d better go and see to en, ” and he trotted after the stranger. [/]
[Narrator] He met Mrs. Hall in the passage. [/]
[TheStranger] “ Carrier ’s darg, ” he said “ bit en. ” [/]
[Narrator] He went straight upstairs, and the stranger ’s door being ajar, he pushed it open and was entering without any ceremony, being of a naturally sympathetic turn of mind. [/]
[Narrator] The blind was down and the room dim. [/]
[Narrator] He caught a glimpse of a most singular thing, what seemed a handless arm waving towards him, and a face of three huge indeterminate spots on white, very like the face of a pale pansy. [/]
[Narrator] Then he was struck violently in the chest, hurled back, and the door slammed in his face and locked. [/]
[Narrator] It was so rapid that it gave him no time to observe. [/]
[Narrator] A waving of indecipherable shapes, a blow, and a concussion. [/]
[Narrator] There he stood on the dark little landing, wondering what it might be that he had seen. [/]
[MrsHall] A couple of minutes after, he rejoined the little group that had formed outside the “ Coach and Horses. ” [/]
[Them] There was Fearenside telling about it all over again for the second time; there was Mrs. Hall saying his dog did n’t have no business to bite her guests; there was Huxter, the general dealer from over the road, interrogative; and Sandy Wadgers from the forge, judicial; besides women and children, all of them saying fatuities: “ Would n’t let en bite _ me _, I knows ”; “ ’ Tasn’t right _ have _ such dargs ”; “ Whad ’ _ e _ bite ’ n for, then? ” and so forth. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Hall, staring at them from the steps and listening, found it incredible that he had seen anything so very remarkable happen upstairs. [/]
[Narrator] Besides, his vocabulary was altogether too limited to express his impressions. [/]
[MrHall] “ He do n’t want no help, he says, ” he said in answer to his wife ’s inquiry. [/]
[Narrator] “ We ’d better be a - takin ’ of his luggage in. ” “ He ought to have it cauterised at once, ” said Mr. Huxter; “ especially if it ’s at all inflamed. ” [/]
[ALadyInTheGroup] “ I ’d shoot en, that ’s what I ’d do, ” said a lady in the group. [/]
[Narrator] Suddenly the dog began growling again. [/]
[TheMuffledStranger] “ Come along, ” cried an angry voice in the doorway, and there stood the muffled stranger with his collar turned up, and his hat - brim bent down. [/]
[TheMuffledStranger] “ The sooner you get those things in the better I ’ll be pleased. ” [/]
[Narrator] It is stated by an anonymous bystander that his trousers and gloves had been changed. [/]
[Fearenside] “ Was you hurt, sir? ” said Fearenside. [/]
[Narrator] “ I ’m rare sorry the darg — ” “ Not a bit, ” said the stranger. [/]
[TheStranger] “ Never broke the skin. [/]
[TheStranger] Hurry up with those things. ” [/]
[Narrator] He then swore to himself, so Mr. Hall asserts. [/]
[Narrator] Directly the first crate was, in accordance with his directions, carried into the parlour, the stranger flung himself upon it with extraordinary eagerness, and began to unpack it, scattering the straw with an utter disregard of Mrs. Hall ’s carpet. [/]
[Narrator] And from it he began to produce bottles — little fat bottles containing powders, small and slender bottles containing coloured and white fluids, fluted blue bottles labeled Poison, bottles with round bodies and slender necks, large green - glass bottles, large white - glass bottles, bottles with glass stoppers and frosted labels, bottles with fine corks, bottles with bungs, bottles with wooden caps, wine bottles, salad - oil bottles — putting them in rows on the chiffonnier, on the mantel, on the table under the window, round the floor, on the bookshelf — everywhere. [/]
[Narrator] The chemist ’s shop in Bramblehurst could not boast half so many. [/]
[Narrator] Quite a sight it was. [/]
[Narrator] Crate after crate yielded bottles, until all six were empty and the table high with straw; the only things that came out of these crates besides the bottles were a number of test - tubes and a carefully packed balance. [/]
[Narrator] And directly the crates were unpacked, the stranger went to the window and set to work, not troubling in the least about the litter of straw, the fire which had gone out, the box of books outside, nor for the trunks and other luggage that had gone upstairs. [/]
[Narrator] When Mrs. Hall took his dinner in to him, he was already so absorbed in his work, pouring little drops out of the bottles into test - tubes, that he did not hear her until she had swept away the bulk of the straw and put the tray on the table, with some little emphasis perhaps, seeing the state that the floor was in. [/]
[Narrator] Then he half turned his head and immediately turned it away again. [/]
[Narrator] But she saw he had removed his glasses; they were beside him on the table, and it seemed to her that his eye sockets were extraordinarily hollow. [/]
[Narrator] He put on his spectacles again, and then turned and faced her. [/]
[Narrator] She was about to complain of the straw on the floor when he anticipated her. [/]
[TheMuffledStranger] “ I wish you would n’t come in without knocking, ” he said in the tone of abnormal exasperation that seemed so characteristic of him. [/]
[TheMuffledStranger] “ I knocked, but seemingly — ” “ Perhaps you did. [/]
[TheMuffledStranger] But in my investigations — my really very urgent and necessary investigations — the slightest disturbance, the jar of a door — I must ask you — ” “ Certainly, sir. [/]
[TheMuffledStranger] You can turn the lock if you ’re like that, you know. [/]
[TheMuffledStranger] Any time. ” [/]
[TheStranger] “ A very good idea, ” said the stranger. [/]
[TheMuffledStranger] “ This stror, sir, if I might make so bold as to remark — ” “ Do n’t. [/]
[TheMuffledStranger] If the straw makes trouble put it down in the bill. ” [/]
[Narrator] And he mumbled at her — words suspiciously like curses. [/]
[Narrator] He was so odd, standing there, so aggressive and explosive, bottle in one hand and test - tube in the other, that Mrs. Hall was quite alarmed. [/]
[Narrator] But she was a resolute woman. [/]
[MrsHall] “ In which case, I should like to know, sir, what you consider — ” “ A shilling — put down a shilling. [/]
[MrsHall] Surely a shilling ’s enough? ” “ So be it, ” said Mrs. Hall, taking up the table - cloth and beginning to spread it over the table. [/]
[MrsHall] “ If you ’re satisfied, of course — ” He turned and sat down, with his coat - collar toward her. [/]
[Narrator] All the afternoon he worked with the door locked and, as Mrs. Hall testifies, for the most part in silence. [/]
[Narrator] But once there was a concussion and a sound of bottles ringing together as though the table had been hit, and the smash of a bottle flung violently down, and then a rapid pacing athwart the room. [/]
[MrsHall] Fearing “ something was the matter, ” she went to the door and listened, not caring to knock. [/]
[TheMuffledStranger] “ I ca n’t go on, ” he was raving. [/]
[TheMuffledStranger] “ I _ ca n’t _ go on. [/]
[TheMuffledStranger] Three hundred thousand, four hundred thousand! [/]
[TheMuffledStranger] The huge multitude! [/]
[TheMuffledStranger] Cheated! [/]
[TheMuffledStranger] All my life it may take me! .. . [/]
[TheMuffledStranger] Patience! [/]
[TheMuffledStranger] Patience indeed! .. . [/]
[TheMuffledStranger] Fool! fool! ” [/]
[Narrator] There was a noise of hobnails on the bricks in the bar, and Mrs. Hall had very reluctantly to leave the rest of his soliloquy. [/]
[Narrator] When she returned the room was silent again, save for the faint crepitation of his chair and the occasional clink of a bottle. [/]
[Narrator] It was all over; the stranger had resumed work. [/]
[Narrator] When she took in his tea she saw broken glass in the corner of the room under the concave mirror, and a golden stain that had been carelessly wiped. [/]
[Narrator] She called attention to it. [/]
[HerVisitor] “ Put it down in the bill, ” snapped her visitor. [/]
[TheMuffledStranger] “ For God ’s sake do n’t worry me. [/]
[TheMuffledStranger] If there ’s damage done, put it down in the bill, ” and he went on ticking a list in the exercise book before him. [/]
[Fearenside] “ I ’ll tell you something, ” said Fearenside, mysteriously. [/]
[Narrator] It was late in the afternoon, and they were in the little beer - shop of Iping Hanger. [/]
[Henfrey] “ Well? ” said Teddy Henfrey. [/]

[Henfrey] Well — he ’s black. [/]

[Fearenside] And it ’s the common way with horses, as any one can see. ” CHAPTER IV. [/]
[Narrator] MR. CUSS INTERVIEWS [/]
[Narrator] THE STRANGER I have told the circumstances of the stranger ’s arrival in Iping with a certain fulness of detail, in order that the curious impression he created may be understood by the reader. [/]
[Narrator] But excepting two odd incidents, the circumstances of his stay until the extraordinary day of the club festival may be passed over very cursorily. [/]
[Narrator] There were a number of skirmishes with Mrs. Hall on matters of domestic discipline, but in every case until late April, when the first signs of penury began, he over - rode her by the easy expedient of an extra payment. [/]
[Narrator] Hall did not like him, and whenever he dared he talked of the advisability of getting rid of him; but he showed his dislike chiefly by concealing it ostentatiously, and avoiding his visitor as much as possible. [/]
[MrsHall] “ Wait till the summer, ” said Mrs. Hall sagely, “ when the artisks are beginning to come. [/]
[MrsHall] Then we ’ll see. [/]
[MrsHall] He may be a bit overbearing, but bills settled punctual is bills settled punctual, whatever you ’d like to say. ” [/]
[Narrator] The stranger did not go to church, and indeed made no difference between Sunday and the irreligious days, even in costume. [/]
[Narrator] He worked, as Mrs. Hall thought, very fitfully. [/]
[Narrator] Some days he would come down early and be continuously busy. [/]
[Narrator] On others he would rise late, pace his room, fretting audibly for hours together, smoke, sleep in the armchair by the fire. [/]
[Narrator] Communication with the world beyond the village he had none. [/]
[Narrator] His temper continued very uncertain; for the most part his manner was that of a man suffering under almost unendurable provocation, and once or twice things were snapped, torn, crushed, or broken in spasmodic gusts of violence. [/]
[Narrator] He seemed under a chronic irritation of the greatest intensity. [/]
[Narrator] His habit of talking to himself in a low voice grew steadily upon him, but though Mrs. Hall listened conscientiously she could make neither head nor tail of what she heard. [/]
[Narrator] He rarely went abroad by daylight, but at twilight he would go out muffled up invisibly, whether the weather were cold or not, and he chose the loneliest paths and those most overshadowed by trees and banks. [/]
[Henfrey] His goggling spectacles and ghastly bandaged face under the penthouse of his hat, came with a disagreeable suddenness out of the darkness upon one or two home - going labourers, and Teddy Henfrey, tumbling out of the “ Scarlet Coat ” one night, at half - past nine, was scared shamefully by the stranger ’s skull - like head (he was walking hat in hand) lit by the sudden light of the opened inn door. [/]
[Narrator] Such children as saw him at nightfall dreamt of bogies, and it seemed doubtful whether he disliked boys more than they disliked him, or the reverse; but there was certainly a vivid enough dislike on either side. [/]
[Narrator] It was inevitable that a person of so remarkable an appearance and bearing should form a frequent topic in such a village as Iping. [/]
[Narrator] Opinion was greatly divided about his occupation. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Hall was sensitive on the point. [/]
[MrsHall] When questioned, she explained very carefully that he was an “ experimental investigator, ” going gingerly over the syllables as one who dreads pitfalls. [/]
[MrsHall] When asked what an experimental investigator was, she would say with a touch of superiority that most educated people knew such things as that, and would thus explain that he “ discovered things. ” [/]
[Narrator] Her visitor had had an accident, she said, which temporarily discoloured his face and hands, and being of a sensitive disposition, he was averse to any public notice of the fact. [/]
[Narrator] Out of her hearing there was a view largely entertained that he was a criminal trying to escape from justice by wrapping himself up so as to conceal himself altogether from the eye of the police. [/]
[Narrator] This idea sprang from the brain of Mr. Teddy Henfrey. [/]
[Narrator] No crime of any magnitude dating from the middle or end of February was known to have occurred. [/]
[Narrator] Elaborated in the imagination of Mr. Gould, the probationary assistant in the National School, this theory took the form that the stranger was an Anarchist in disguise, preparing explosives, and he resolved to undertake such detective operations as his time permitted. [/]
[Narrator] These consisted for the most part in looking very hard at the stranger whenever they met, or in asking people who had never seen the stranger, leading questions about him. [/]
[Narrator] But he detected nothing. [/]
[SilasDurgan] Another school of opinion followed Mr. Fearenside, and either accepted the piebald view or some modification of it; as, for instance, Silas Durgan, who was heard to assert that “ if he chooses to show enself at fairs he ’d make his fortune in no time, ” and being a bit of a theologian, compared the stranger to the man with the one talent. [/]
[Narrator] Yet another view explained the entire matter by regarding the stranger as a harmless lunatic. [/]
[Narrator] That had the advantage of accounting for everything straight away. [/]
[Narrator] Between these main groups there were waverers and compromisers. [/]
[Narrator] Sussex folk have few superstitions, and it was only after the events of early April that the thought of the supernatural was first whispered in the village. [/]
[Narrator] Even then it was only credited among the women folk. [/]
[Narrator] But whatever they thought of him, people in Iping, on the whole, agreed in disliking him. [/]
[Narrator] His irritability, though it might have been comprehensible to an urban brain - worker, was an amazing thing to these quiet Sussex villagers. [/]
[Narrator] The frantic gesticulations they surprised now and then, the headlong pace after nightfall that swept him upon them round quiet corners, the inhuman bludgeoning of all tentative advances of curiosity, the taste for twilight that led to the closing of doors, the pulling down of blinds, the extinction of candles and lamps — who could agree with such goings on? [/]
[Narrator] They drew aside as he passed down the village, and when he had gone by, young humourists would up with coat - collars and down with hat - brims, and go pacing nervously after him in imitation of his occult bearing. [/]
[MissStatchell] There was a song popular at that time called “ The Bogey Man ”. [/]
[Narrator] Miss Statchell sang it at the schoolroom concert (in aid of the church lamps), and thereafter whenever one or two of the villagers were gathered together and the stranger appeared, a bar or so of this tune, more or less sharp or flat, was whistled in the midst of them. [/]
[AlsoBelatedLittleChildren] Also belated little children would call “ Bogey Man! ” after him, and make off tremulously elated. [/]
[Narrator] Cuss, the general practitioner, was devoured by curiosity. [/]
[Narrator] The bandages excited his professional interest, the report of the thousand and one bottles aroused his jealous regard. [/]
[Narrator] All through April and May he coveted an opportunity of talking to the stranger, and at last, towards Whitsuntide, he could stand it no longer, but hit upon the subscription - list for a village nurse as an excuse. [/]
[Narrator] He was surprised to find that Mr. Hall did not know his guest ’s name. [/]
[Narrator] “ He give a name, ” said Mrs. Hall — an assertion which was quite unfounded—“but I did n’t rightly hear it. ” [/]
[I] She thought it seemed so silly not to know the man ’s name. [/]
[Narrator] Cuss rapped at the parlour door and entered. [/]
[Narrator] There was a fairly audible imprecation from within. [/]
[Cuss] “ Pardon my intrusion, ” said Cuss, and then the door closed and cut Mrs. Hall off from the rest of the conversation. [/]
[Narrator] She could hear the murmur of voices for the next ten minutes, then a cry of surprise, a stirring of feet, a chair flung aside, a bark of laughter, quick steps to the door, and Cuss appeared, his face white, his eyes staring over his shoulder. [/]
[Narrator] He left the door open behind him, and without looking at her strode across the hall and went down the steps, and she heard his feet hurrying along the road. [/]
[Narrator] He carried his hat in his hand. [/]
[Narrator] She stood behind the door, looking at the open door of the parlour. [/]
[Narrator] Then she heard the stranger laughing quietly, and then his footsteps came across the room. [/]
[Narrator] She could not see his face where she stood. [/]
[Narrator] The parlour door slammed, and the place was silent again. [/]
[Narrator] Cuss went straight up the village to Bunting the vicar. [/]
[Cuss] “ Am I mad? ” Cuss began abruptly, as he entered the shabby little study. [/]
[Narrator] “ Do I look like an insane person? ” “ What ’s happened? ” said the vicar, putting the ammonite on the loose sheets of his forth - coming sermon. [/]
[Cuss] “ That chap at the inn — ” “ Well? ” “ Give me something to drink, ” said Cuss, and he sat down. [/]
[Narrator] When his nerves had been steadied by a glass of cheap sherry — the only drink the good vicar had available — he told him of the interview he had just had. [/]
[Cuss] “ Went in, ” he gasped, “ and began to demand a subscription for that Nurse Fund. [/]
[Cuss] He ’d stuck his hands in his pockets as I came in, and he sat down lumpily in his chair. [/]
[Cuss] Sniffed. [/]
[Cuss] I told him I ’d heard he took an interest in scientific things. [/]
[Cuss] He said yes. [/]
[Cuss] Sniffed again. [/]
[Cuss] Kept on sniffing all the time; evidently recently caught an infernal cold. [/]
[Cuss] No wonder, wrapped up like that! [/]
[Cuss] I developed the nurse idea, and all the while kept my eyes open. [/]
[Cuss] Bottles — chemicals — everywhere. [/]
[Cuss] Balance, test - tubes in stands, and a smell of — evening primrose. [/]

[Cuss] Said he ’d consider it. [/]

[Cuss] Said he was. [/]

[Cuss] Got quite cross. [/]

[Cuss] Was it medical? [/]
[Cuss] ‘ Damn you! [/]

[Cuss] Five ingredients. [/]

[Cuss] Rushed towards it just as it whisked up the chimney. [/]

[Narrator] Mr. Bunting thought it over. [/]

[Narrator] He looked very wise and grave indeed. [/]
[MrBunting] “ It ’s really, ” said Mr. Bunting with judicial emphasis, “ a most remarkable story. ” CHAPTER V. THE BURGLARY AT THE VICARAGE The facts of the burglary at the vicarage came to us chiefly through the medium of the vicar and his wife. [/]
[Narrator] It occurred in the small hours of Whit Monday, the day devoted in Iping to the Club festivities. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Bunting, it seems, woke up suddenly in the stillness that comes before the dawn, with the strong impression that the door of their bedroom had opened and closed. [/]
[Narrator] She did not arouse her husband at first, but sat up in bed listening. [/]
[Narrator] She then distinctly heard the pad, pad, pad of bare feet coming out of the adjoining dressing - room and walking along the passage towards the staircase. [/]
[Narrator] As soon as she felt assured of this, she aroused the Rev. Mr. Bunting as quietly as possible. [/]
[Narrator] He did not strike a light, but putting on his spectacles, her dressing - gown and his bath slippers, he went out on the landing to listen. [/]
[Narrator] He heard quite distinctly a fumbling going on at his study desk down - stairs, and then a violent sneeze. [/]
[Narrator] At that he returned to his bedroom, armed himself with the most obvious weapon, the poker, and descended the staircase as noiselessly as possible. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Bunting came out on the landing. [/]
[Narrator] The hour was about four, and the ultimate darkness of the night was past. [/]
[Narrator] There was a faint shimmer of light in the hall, but the study doorway yawned impenetrably black. [/]
[Narrator] Everything was still except the faint creaking of the stairs under Mr. Bunting ’s tread, and the slight movements in the study. [/]
[Narrator] Then something snapped, the drawer was opened, and there was a rustle of papers. [/]
[Narrator] Then came an imprecation, and a match was struck and the study was flooded with yellow light. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Bunting was now in the hall, and through the crack of the door he could see the desk and the open drawer and a candle burning on the desk. [/]
[Narrator] But the robber he could not see. [/]
[Narrator] He stood there in the hall undecided what to do, and Mrs. Bunting, her face white and intent, crept slowly downstairs after him. [/]
[Narrator] One thing kept Mr. Bunting ’s courage; the persuasion that this burglar was a resident in the village. [/]
[Narrator] They heard the chink of money, and realised that the robber had found the housekeeping reserve of gold — two pounds ten in half sovereigns altogether. [/]
[Narrator] At that sound Mr. Bunting was nerved to abrupt action. [/]
[Narrator] Gripping the poker firmly, he rushed into the room, closely followed by Mrs. Bunting. [/]
[MrBunting] “ Surrender! ” cried Mr. Bunting, fiercely, and then stooped amazed. [/]
[Narrator] Apparently the room was perfectly empty. [/]
[Narrator] Yet their conviction that they had, that very moment, heard somebody moving in the room had amounted to a certainty. [/]
[Narrator] For half a minute, perhaps, they stood gaping, then Mrs. Bunting went across the room and looked behind the screen, while Mr. Bunting, by a kindred impulse, peered under the desk. [/]
[Narrator] Then Mrs. Bunting turned back the window - curtains, and Mr. Bunting looked up the chimney and probed it with the poker. [/]
[Narrator] Then Mrs. Bunting scrutinised the waste - paper basket and Mr. Bunting opened the lid of the coal - scuttle. [/]
[Narrator] Then they came to a stop and stood with eyes interrogating each other. [/]
[MrBunting] “ I could have sworn — ” said Mr. Bunting. [/]
[MrBunting] “ The candle! ” said Mr. Bunting. [/]
[Narrator] “ Who lit the candle? ” “ The drawer! ” said Mrs. Bunting. [/]
[MrsBunting] “ And the money ’s gone! ” She went hastily to the doorway. [/]
[MrsBunting] “ Of all the strange occurrences — ” There was a violent sneeze in the passage. [/]
[Narrator] They rushed out, and as they did so the kitchen door slammed. [/]
[MrBunting] “ Bring the candle, ” said Mr. Bunting, and led the way. [/]
[Narrator] They both heard a sound of bolts being hastily shot back. [/]
[Narrator] As he opened the kitchen door he saw through the scullery that the back door was just opening, and the faint light of early dawn displayed the dark masses of the garden beyond. [/]
[Narrator] He is certain that nothing went out of the door. [/]
[Narrator] It opened, stood open for a moment, and then closed with a slam. [/]
[Narrator] As it did so, the candle Mrs. Bunting was carrying from the study flickered and flared. [/]
[Narrator] It was a minute or more before they entered the kitchen. [/]
[Narrator] The place was empty. [/]
[Narrator] They refastened the back door, examined the kitchen, pantry, and scullery thoroughly, and at last went down into the cellar. [/]
[Narrator] There was not a soul to be found in the house, search as they would. [/]
[Narrator] Daylight found the vicar and his wife, a quaintly - costumed little couple, still marvelling about on their own ground floor by the unnecessary light of a guttering candle. CHAPTER VI. [/]
[Narrator] THE FURNITURE THAT WENT MAD [/]
[Narrator] Now it happened that in the early hours of Whit Monday, before Millie was hunted out for the day, Mr. Hall and Mrs. Hall both rose and went noiselessly down into the cellar. [/]
[Narrator] Their business there was of a private nature, and had something to do with the specific gravity of their beer. [/]
[Narrator] They had hardly entered the cellar when Mrs. Hall found she had forgotten to bring down a bottle of sarsaparilla from their joint - room. [/]
[Narrator] As she was the expert and principal operator in this affair, Hall very properly went upstairs for it. [/]
[Narrator] On the landing he was surprised to see that the stranger ’s door was ajar. [/]
[Narrator] He went on into his own room and found the bottle as he had been directed. [/]
[Narrator] But returning with the bottle, he noticed that the bolts of the front door had been shot back, that the door was in fact simply on the latch. [/]
[Narrator] And with a flash of inspiration he connected this with the stranger ’s room upstairs and the suggestions of Mr. Teddy Henfrey. [/]
[Narrator] He distinctly remembered holding the candle while Mrs. Hall shot these bolts overnight. [/]
[Narrator] At the sight he stopped, gaping, then with the bottle still in his hand went upstairs again. [/]
[Narrator] He rapped at the stranger ’s door. [/]
[Narrator] There was no answer. [/]
[Narrator] He rapped again; then pushed the door wide open and entered. [/]
[Narrator] It was as he expected. [/]
[Narrator] The bed, the room also, was empty. [/]
[Narrator] And what was stranger, even to his heavy intelligence, on the bedroom chair and along the rail of the bed were scattered the garments, the only garments so far as he knew, and the bandages of their guest. [/]
[Narrator] His big slouch hat even was cocked jauntily over the bed - post. [/]
[Narrator] As Hall stood there he heard his wife ’s voice coming out of the depth of the cellar, with that rapid telescoping of the syllables and interrogative cocking up of the final words to a high note, by which the West Sussex villager is wo nt to indicate a brisk impatience. [/]
[TheWestSussexVillager] “ George! [/]
[TheWestSussexVillager] You gart whad a wand? ” At that he turned and hurried down to her. [/]

[Narrator] Hall, still holding the bottle, went first. [/]
[MrHall] “ If ’ e en’t there, ” he said, “ ’ is close are. [/]
[MrHall] And what ’s ’ e doin’ ’ ithout ’ is close, then? ’ [/]
[MrHall] Tas a most curious business. ” [/]
[Narrator] As they came up the cellar steps they both, it was afterwards ascertained, fancied they heard the front door open and shut, but seeing it closed and nothing there, neither said a word to the other about it at the time. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Hall passed her husband in the passage and ran on first upstairs. [/]
[Narrator] Someone sneezed on the staircase. [/]
[Narrator] Hall, following six steps behind, thought that he heard her sneeze. [/]
[Narrator] She, going on first, was under the impression that Hall was sneezing. [/]
[Narrator] She flung open the door and stood regarding the room. [/]
[MrsHall] “ Of all the curious! ” she said. [/]
[Narrator] She heard a sniff close behind her head as it seemed, and turning, was surprised to see Hall a dozen feet off on the topmost stair. [/]
[Narrator] But in another moment he was beside her. [/]
[Narrator] She bent forward and put her hand on the pillow and then under the clothes. [/]
[MrsHall] “ Cold, ” she said. [/]
[MrsHall] “ He ’s been up this hour or more. ” [/]
[Narrator] As she did so, a most extraordinary thing happened. [/]
[Narrator] The bed - clothes gathered themselves together, leapt up suddenly into a sort of peak, and then jumped headlong over the bottom rail. [/]
[Narrator] It was exactly as if a hand had clutched them in the centre and flung them aside. [/]
[Narrator] Immediately after, the stranger ’s hat hopped off the bed - post, described a whirling flight in the air through the better part of a circle, and then dashed straight at Mrs. Hall ’s face. [/]
[Narrator] Then as swiftly came the sponge from the washstand; and then the chair, flinging the stranger ’s coat and trousers carelessly aside, and laughing drily in a voice singularly like the stranger ’s, turned itself up with its four legs at Mrs. Hall, seemed to take aim at her for a moment, and charged at her. [/]
[Narrator] She screamed and turned, and then the chair legs came gently but firmly against her back and impelled her and Hall out of the room. [/]
[Narrator] The door slammed violently and was locked. [/]
[Narrator] The chair and bed seemed to be executing a dance of triumph for a moment, and then abruptly everything was still. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Hall was left almost in a fainting condition in Mr. Hall ’s arms on the landing. [/]
[Narrator] It was with the greatest difficulty that Mr. Hall and Millie, who had been roused by her scream of alarm, succeeded in getting her downstairs, and applying the restoratives customary in such cases. [/]

[Narrator] The Anglo - Saxon genius for parliamentary government asserted itself; there was a great deal of talk and no decisive action. [/]

[/]
[Narrator] He came down stiffly and slowly, staring all the time; he walked across the passage staring, then stopped. [/]

[Narrator] he said, and their eyes followed the direction of his gloved finger and saw a bottle of sarsaparilla hard by the cellar door. [/]

[Narrator] CHAPTER VII. [/]
[TheStranger] THE UNVEILING OF THE STRANGER The stranger went into the little parlour of the “ Coach and Horses ” about half - past five in the morning, and there he remained until near midday, the blinds down, the door shut, and none, after Hall ’s repulse, venturing near him. [/]
[Narrator] All that time he must have fasted. [/]
[Narrator] Thrice he rang his bell, the third time furiously and continuously, but no one answered him. [/]
[MrsHall] “ Him and his ‘ go to the devil ’ indeed! ” said Mrs. Hall. [/]
[Narrator] Presently came an imperfect rumour of the burglary at the vicarage, and two and two were put together. [/]
[Narrator] Hall, assisted by Wadgers, went off to find Mr. Shuckleforth, the magistrate, and take his advice. [/]
[Narrator] No one ventured upstairs. [/]
[Narrator] How the stranger occupied himself is unknown. [/]
[Narrator] Now and then he would stride violently up and down, and twice came an outburst of curses, a tearing of paper, and a violent smashing of bottles. [/]
[Narrator] The little group of scared but curious people increased. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Huxter came over; some gay young fellows resplendent in black ready - made jackets and _ piqué _ paper ties — for it was Whit Monday — joined the group with confused interrogations. [/]
[Narrator] Young Archie Harker distinguished himself by going up the yard and trying to peep under the window - blinds. [/]
[Narrator] He could see nothing, but gave reason for supposing that he did, and others of the Iping youth presently joined him. [/]
[Narrator] It was the finest of all possible Whit Mondays, and down the village street stood a row of nearly a dozen booths, a shooting gallery, and on the grass by the forge were three yellow and chocolate waggons and some picturesque strangers of both sexes putting up a cocoanut shy. [/]
[Narrator] The gentlemen wore blue jerseys, the ladies white aprons and quite fashionable hats with heavy plumes. [/]
[Wodger] Wodger, of the “ Purple Fawn, ” and Mr. Jaggers, the cobbler, who also sold old second - hand ordinary bicycles, were stretching a string of union - jacks and royal ensigns (which had originally celebrated the first Victorian Jubilee) across the road. [/]
[Narrator] And inside, in the artificial darkness of the parlour, into which only one thin jet of sunlight penetrated, the stranger, hungry we must suppose, and fearful, hidden in his uncomfortable hot wrappings, pored through his dark glasses upon his paper or chinked his dirty little bottles, and occasionally swore savagely at the boys, audible if invisible, outside the windows. [/]
[Narrator] In the corner by the fireplace lay the fragments of half a dozen smashed bottles, and a pungent twang of chlorine tainted the air. [/]
[Narrator] So much we know from what was heard at the time and from what was subsequently seen in the room. [/]

People down the village heard shouts and shrieks, and looking up the street saw the “ Coach and Horses ” violently firing out its humanity. [/]

[Narrator] Then he stared. [/]

[Narrator] Several other of the men folks had now entered the room, so that it was closely crowded. [/]

[/]

[Narrator] A perfect Babel of noises they made. [/]

[Narrator] The hitting continued. [/]
[Narrator] Phipps, the Unitarian, had a front tooth broken, and Henfrey was injured in the cartilage of his ear. [/]
[Narrator] Jaffers was struck under the jaw, and, turning, caught at something that intervened between him and Huxter in the mêlée, and prevented their coming together. [/]
[Narrator] He felt a muscular chest, and in another moment the whole mass of struggling, excited men shot out into the crowded hall. [/]
[Jaffers] “ I got him! ” [/]
[Narrator] shouted Jaffers, choking and reeling through them all, and wrestling with purple face and swelling veins against his unseen enemy. [/]
[Narrator] Men staggered right and left as the extraordinary conflict swayed swiftly towards the house door, and went spinning down the half - dozen steps of the inn. [/]
[Narrator] Jaffers cried in a strangled voice — holding tight, nevertheless, and making play with his knee — spun around, and fell heavily undermost with his head on the gravel. [/]
[Narrator] Only then did his fingers relax. [/]
[AYoungFellow] There were excited cries of “ Hold him! ” [/]
[AYoungFellow] “ Invisible! ” and so forth, and a young fellow, a stranger in the place whose name did not come to light, rushed in at once, caught something, missed his hold, and fell over the constable ’s prostrate body. [/]
[Narrator] Half - way across the road a woman screamed as something pushed by her; a dog, kicked apparently, yelped and ran howling into Huxter ’s yard, and with that the transit of the Invisible Man was accomplished. [/]
[Narrator] For a space people stood amazed and gesticulating, and then came panic, and scattered them abroad through the village as a gust scatters dead leaves. [/]
[Narrator] But Jaffers lay quite still, face upward and knees bent, at the foot of the steps of the inn. CHAPTER VIII. [/]
[Narrator] IN TRANSIT The eighth chapter is exceedingly brief, and relates that Gibbons, the amateur naturalist of the district, while lying out on the spacious open downs without a soul within a couple of miles of him, as he thought, and almost dozing, heard close to him the sound as of a man coughing, sneezing, and then swearing savagely to himself; and looking, beheld nothing. [/]
[Narrator] Yet the voice was indisputable. [/]
[Narrator] It continued to swear with that breadth and variety that distinguishes the swearing of a cultivated man. [/]
[Narrator] It grew to a climax, diminished again, and died away in the distance, going as it seemed to him in the direction of Adderdean. [/]
[Narrator] It lifted to a spasmodic sneeze and ended. [/]
[Narrator] Gibbons had heard nothing of the morning ’s occurrences, but the phenomenon was so striking and disturbing that his philosophical tranquillity vanished; he got up hastily, and hurried down the steepness of the hill towards the village, as fast as he could go. [/]
[Narrator] CHAPTER IX. MR. [/]
[Narrator] THOMAS MARVEL You must picture Mr. Thomas Marvel as a person of copious, flexible visage, a nose of cylindrical protrusion, a liquorish, ample, fluctuating mouth, and a beard of bristling eccentricity. [/]
[Narrator] His figure inclined to embonpoint; his short limbs accentuated this inclination. [/]
[Narrator] He wore a furry silk hat, and the frequent substitution of twine and shoe - laces for buttons, apparent at critical points of his costume, marked a man essentially bachelor. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Thomas Marvel was sitting with his feet in a ditch by the roadside over the down towards Adderdean, about a mile and a half out of Iping. [/]
[Narrator] His feet, save for socks of irregular open - work, were bare, his big toes were broad, and pricked like the ears of a watchful dog. [/]
[Narrator] In a leisurely manner — he did everything in a leisurely manner — he was contemplating trying on a pair of boots. [/]
[Narrator] They were the soundest boots he had come across for a long time, but too large for him; whereas the ones he had were, in dry weather, a very comfortable fit, but too thin - soled for damp. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Thomas Marvel hated roomy shoes, but then he hated damp. [/]
[Narrator] He had never properly thought out which he hated most, and it was a pleasant day, and there was nothing better to do. [/]
[Narrator] So he put the four shoes in a graceful group on the turf and looked at them. [/]
[Narrator] And seeing them there among the grass and springing agrimony, it suddenly occurred to him that both pairs were exceedingly ugly to see. [/]
[Narrator] He was not at all startled by a voice behind him. [/]
[TheVoice] “ They ’re boots, anyhow, ” said the Voice. [/]
[Narrator] “ They are — charity boots, ” said Mr. Thomas Marvel, with his head on one side regarding them distastefully; “ and which is the ugliest pair in the whole blessed universe, I ’m darned if I know! ” “ H’m, ” said the Voice. [/]
[MrMarvel] “ I ’ve worn worse — in fact, I ’ve worn none. [/]

[MrMarvel] Look at ’em! [/]

[MrMarvel] It beats it. ” [/]

[Narrator] Scepticism suddenly reared its head — rather nervous scepticism, not at all assured of its back, but scepticism nevertheless. [/]
[Narrator] It is so much easier not to believe in an invisible man; and those who had actually seen him dissolve into air, or felt the strength of his arm, could be counted on the fingers of two hands. [/]
[TheseWitnesses] And of these witnesses Mr. Wadgers was presently missing, having retired impregnably behind the bolts and bars of his own house, and Jaffers was lying stunned in the parlour of the “ Coach and Horses. ” [/]
[Narrator] Great and strange ideas transcending experience often have less effect upon men and women than smaller, more tangible considerations. [/]
[Narrator] Iping was gay with bunting, and everybody was in gala dress. [/]
[Narrator] Whit Monday had been looked forward to for a month or more. [/]
[Narrator] By the afternoon even those who believed in the Unseen were beginning to resume their little amusements in a tentative fashion, on the supposition that he had quite gone away, and with the sceptics he was already a jest. [/]
[Narrator] But people, sceptics and believers alike, were remarkably sociable all that day. [/]
[Narrator] Haysman ’s meadow was gay with a tent, in which Mrs. Bunting and other ladies were preparing tea, while, without, the Sunday - school children ran races and played games under the noisy guidance of the curate and the Misses Cuss and Sackbut. [/]
[Narrator] No doubt there was a slight uneasiness in the air, but people for the most part had the sense to conceal whatever imaginative qualms they experienced. [/]
[Narrator] On the village green an inclined strong [rope?], down which, clinging the while to a pulley - swung handle, one could be hurled violently against a sack at the other end, came in for considerable favour among the adolescents, as also did the swings and the cocoanut shies. [/]
[Narrator] There was also promenading, and the steam organ attached to a small roundabout filled the air with a pungent flavour of oil and with equally pungent music. [/]
[Narrator] Members of the club, who had attended church in the morning, were splendid in badges of pink and green, and some of the gayer - minded had also adorned their bowler hats with brilliant - coloured favours of ribbon. [/]
[Narrator] Old Fletcher, whose conceptions of holiday - making were severe, was visible through the jasmine about his window or through the open door (whichever way you chose to look), poised delicately on a plank supported on two chairs, and whitewashing the ceiling of his front room. [/]
[Narrator] About four o’clock a stranger entered the village from the direction of the downs. [/]
[Narrator] He was a short, stout person in an extraordinarily shabby top hat, and he appeared to be very much out of breath. [/]
[Narrator] His cheeks were alternately limp and tightly puffed. [/]
[Narrator] His mottled face was apprehensive, and he moved with a sort of reluctant alacrity. [/]
[AStranger] He turned the corner of the church, and directed his way to the “ Coach and Horses. ” [/]
[Narrator] Among others old Fletcher remembers seeing him, and indeed the old gentleman was so struck by his peculiar agitation that he inadvertently allowed a quantity of whitewash to run down the brush into the sleeve of his coat while regarding him. [/]
[Narrator] This stranger, to the perceptions of the proprietor of the cocoanut shy, appeared to be talking to himself, and Mr. Huxter remarked the same thing. [/]
[AStranger] He stopped at the foot of the “ Coach and Horses ” steps, and, according to Mr. Huxter, appeared to undergo a severe internal struggle before he could induce himself to enter the house. [/]
[Narrator] Finally he marched up the steps, and was seen by Mr. Huxter to turn to the left and open the door of the parlour. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Huxter heard voices from within the room and from the bar apprising the man of his error. [/]
[MrsHall] “ That room ’s private! ” said Hall, and the stranger shut the door clumsily and went into the bar. [/]
[Narrator] In the course of a few minutes he reappeared, wiping his lips with the back of his hand with an air of quiet satisfaction that somehow impressed Mr. Huxter as assumed. [/]
[Narrator] He stood looking about him for some moments, and then Mr. Huxter saw him walk in an oddly furtive manner towards the gates of the yard, upon which the parlour window opened. [/]
[Narrator] The stranger, after some hesitation, leant against one of the gate - posts, produced a short clay pipe, and prepared to fill it. [/]
[Narrator] His fingers trembled while doing so. [/]
[Narrator] He lit it clumsily, and folding his arms began to smoke in a languid attitude, an attitude which his occasional glances up the yard altogether belied. [/]
[Narrator] All this Mr. Huxter saw over the canisters of the tobacco window, and the singularity of the man ’s behaviour prompted him to maintain his observation. [/]
[Narrator] Presently the stranger stood up abruptly and put his pipe in his pocket. [/]
[Narrator] Then he vanished into the yard. [/]
[Narrator] Forthwith Mr. Huxter, conceiving he was witness of some petty larceny, leapt round his counter and ran out into the road to intercept the thief. [/]
[Narrator] As he did so, Mr. Marvel reappeared, his hat askew, a big bundle in a blue table - cloth in one hand, and three books tied together — as it proved afterwards with the Vicar ’s braces — in the other. [/]
[Narrator] Directly he saw Huxter he gave a sort of gasp, and turning sharply to the left, began to run. [/]
[MrHuxter] “ Stop, thief! ” cried Huxter, and set off after him. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Huxter ’s sensations were vivid but brief. [/]
[Narrator] He saw the man just before him and spurting briskly for the church corner and the hill road. [/]
[Narrator] He saw the village flags and festivities beyond, and a face or so turned towards him. [/]
[MrHuxter] He bawled, “ Stop! ” again. [/]
[Narrator] He had hardly gone ten strides before his shin was caught in some mysterious fashion, and he was no longer running, but flying with inconceivable rapidity through the air. [/]
[Narrator] He saw the ground suddenly close to his face. [/]
[Narrator] The world seemed to splash into a million whirling specks of light, and subsequent proceedings interested him no more. CHAPTER XI. [/]
[TheCoachAndHorses] IN THE “ COACH AND HORSES ” [/]

[TheInvisibleMan] I want clothing — and other accommodation; and I must also have those three books. ” CHAPTER XII. [/]
[Narrator] THE INVISIBLE MAN LOSES HIS TEMPER It is unavoidable that at this point the narrative should break off again, for a certain very painful reason that will presently be apparent. [/]
[Narrator] While these things were going on in the parlour, and while Mr. Huxter was watching Mr. Marvel smoking his pipe against the gate, not a dozen yards away were Mr. Hall and Teddy Henfrey discussing in a state of cloudy puzzlement the one Iping topic. [/]

[Narrator] he said, rushing in. [/]

[Narrator] He heard a frightful struggle in the passage of the inn, and his decision was made. [/]

[Narrator] From the moment when the Invisible Man screamed with rage and Mr. Bunting made his memorable flight up the village, it became impossible to give a consecutive account of affairs in Iping. [/]
[Narrator] Possibly the Invisible Man ’s original intention was simply to cover Marvel ’s retreat with the clothes and books. [/]
[Narrator] But his temper, at no time very good, seems to have gone completely at some chance blow, and forthwith he set to smiting and overthrowing, for the mere satisfaction of hurting. [/]
[Narrator] You must figure the street full of running figures, of doors slamming and fights for hiding - places. [/]
[Narrator] You must figure the tumult suddenly striking on the unstable equilibrium of old Fletcher ’s planks and two chairs — with cataclysmic results. [/]
[Narrator] You must figure an appalled couple caught dismally in a swing. [/]
[Narrator] And then the whole tumultuous rush has passed and the Iping street with its gauds and flags is deserted save for the still raging unseen, and littered with cocoanuts, overthrown canvas screens, and the scattered stock in trade of a sweetstuff stall. [/]
[Narrator] Everywhere there is a sound of closing shutters and shoving bolts, and the only visible humanity is an occasional flitting eye under a raised eyebrow in the corner of a window pane. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] The Invisible Man amused himself for a little while by breaking all the windows in the “ Coach and Horses, ” and then he thrust a street lamp through the parlour window of Mrs. Gribble. [/]
[Narrator] He it must have been who cut the telegraph wire to Adderdean just beyond Higgins ’ cottage on the Adderdean road. [/]
[Narrator] And after that, as his peculiar qualities allowed, he passed out of human perceptions altogether, and he was neither heard, seen, nor felt in Iping any more. [/]
[Narrator] He vanished absolutely. [/]
[Narrator] But it was the best part of two hours before any human being ventured out again into the desolation of Iping street. [/]
[Narrator] CHAPTER XIII. [/]
[Narrator] MR. MARVEL DISCUSSES HIS RESIGNATION [/]
[Narrator] When the dusk was gathering and Iping was just beginning to peep timorously forth again upon the shattered wreckage of its Bank Holiday, a short, thick - set man in a shabby silk hat was marching painfully through the twilight behind the beechwoods on the road to Bramblehurst. [/]
[Narrator] He carried three books bound together by some sort of ornamental elastic ligature, and a bundle wrapped in a blue table - cloth. [/]
[Narrator] His rubicund face expressed consternation and fatigue; he appeared to be in a spasmodic sort of hurry. [/]
[Narrator] He was accompanied by a voice other than his own, and ever and again he winced under the touch of unseen hands. [/]
[Narrator] “ If you give me the slip again, ” said the Voice, “ if you attempt to give me the slip again — ” “ Lord! ” said Mr. Marvel. [/]
