[Narrator] Presently two oblongs of yellow light appeared through the trees, and the square tower of a church loomed through the gloaming. [/]
[TheVoice] “ I shall keep my hand on your shoulder, ” said the Voice, “ all through the village. [/]
[TheVoice] Go straight through and try no foolery. [/]
[TheVoice] It will be the worse for you if you do. ” [/]
[MrMarvel] “ I know that, ” sighed Mr. Marvel, “ I know all that. ” [/]
[Narrator] The unhappy - looking figure in the obsolete silk hat passed up the street of the little village with his burdens, and vanished into the gathering darkness beyond the lights of the windows. [/]
[Narrator] CHAPTER XIV. [/]
[Narrator] AT PORT STOWE Ten o’clock the next morning found Mr. Marvel, unshaven, dirty, and travel - stained, sitting with the books beside him and his hands deep in his pockets, looking very weary, nervous, and uncomfortable, and inflating his cheeks at infrequent intervals, on the bench outside a little inn on the outskirts of Port Stowe. [/]
[Narrator] Beside him were the books, but now they were tied with string. [/]
[Narrator] The bundle had been abandoned in the pine - woods beyond Bramblehurst, in accordance with a change in the plans of the Invisible Man. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Marvel sat on the bench, and although no one took the slightest notice of him, his agitation remained at fever heat. [/]
[Narrator] His hands would go ever and again to his various pockets with a curious nervous fumbling. [/]
[Narrator] When he had been sitting for the best part of an hour, however, an elderly mariner, carrying a newspaper, came out of the inn and sat down beside him. [/]
[TheMariner] “ Pleasant day, ” said the mariner. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Marvel glanced about him with something very like terror. [/]
[MrMarvel] “ Very, ” he said. [/]
[TheMariner] “ Just seasonable weather for the time of year, ” said the mariner, taking no denial. [/]
[MrMarvel] “ Quite, ” said Mr. Marvel. [/]
[Narrator] The mariner produced a toothpick, and (saving his regard) was engrossed thereby for some minutes. [/]
[Narrator] His eyes meanwhile were at liberty to examine Mr. Marvel ’s dusty figure, and the books beside him. [/]
[Narrator] As he had approached Mr. Marvel he had heard a sound like the dropping of coins into a pocket. [/]
[Narrator] He was struck by the contrast of Mr. Marvel ’s appearance with this suggestion of opulence. [/]
[Narrator] Thence his mind wandered back again to a topic that had taken a curiously firm hold of his imagination. [/]
[TheMariner] “ Books? ” [/]
[Narrator] he said suddenly, noisily finishing with the toothpick. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Marvel started and looked at them. [/]
[MrMarvel] “ Oh, yes, ” he said. [/]
[Narrator] “ Yes, they ’re books. ” “ There ’s some extra - ordinary things in books, ” said the mariner. [/]
[MrMarvel] “ I believe you, ” said Mr. Marvel. [/]
[TheMariner] “ And some extra - ordinary things out of ’em, ” said the mariner. [/]
[MrMarvel] “ True likewise, ” said Mr. Marvel. [/]
[Narrator] He eyed his interlocutor, and then glanced about him. [/]
[TheMariner] “ There ’s some extra - ordinary things in newspapers, for example, ” said the mariner. [/]
[TheMariner] “ There are. ” [/]
[TheMariner] “ In _ this _ newspaper, ” said the mariner. [/]
[MrMarvel] “ Ah! ” said Mr. Marvel. [/]
[TheMariner] “ There ’s a story, ” said the mariner, fixing Mr. Marvel with an eye that was firm and deliberate; “ there ’s a story about an Invisible Man, for instance. ” [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Marvel pulled his mouth askew and scratched his cheek and felt his ears glowing. [/]
[MrMarvel] “ What will they be writing next? ” [/]
[Narrator] he asked faintly. [/]
[Narrator] “ Ostria, or America? ” “ Neither, ” said the mariner. [/]
[Narrator] “ _ Here _. ” “ Lord! ” said Mr. Marvel, starting. [/]
[TheMariner] “ When I say _ here _, ” said the mariner, to Mr. Marvel ’s intense relief, “ I do n’t of course mean here in this place, I mean hereabouts. ” [/]
[MrMarvel] “ An Invisible Man! ” said Mr. Marvel. [/]
[Narrator] “ And what ’s _ he _ been up to? ” “ Everything, ” said the mariner, controlling Marvel with his eye, and then amplifying, “ every — blessed — thing. ” [/]
[MrMarvel] “ I ai n’t seen a paper these four days, ” said Marvel. [/]
[TheMariner] “ Iping ’s the place he started at, ” said the mariner. [/]
[MrMarvel] “ In-_deed _! ” said Mr. Marvel. [/]
[TheMariner] “ He started there. [/]
[TheMariner] And where he came from, nobody do n’t seem to know. [/]
[TheMariner] Here it is: ‘ Pe - culiar Story from Iping. ’ [/]
[Narrator] And it says in this paper that the evidence is extra - ordinary strong — extra - ordinary. ” “ Lord! ” said Mr. Marvel. [/]
[TheMariner] “ But then, it ’s an extra - ordinary story. [/]
[TheMariner] There is a clergyman and a medical gent witnesses — saw ’ i m all right and proper — or leastways did n’t see ’ i m. He was staying, it says, at the ‘ Coach an ’ Horses, ’ and no one do n’t seem to have been aware of his misfortune, it says, aware of his misfortune, until in an Altercation in the inn, it says, his bandages on his head was torn off. [/]
[TheMariner] It was then ob - served that his head was invisible. [/]
[TheMariner] Attempts were At Once made to secure him, but casting off his garments, it says, he succeeded in escaping, but not until after a desperate struggle, in which he had inflicted serious injuries, it says, on our worthy and able constable, Mr. J. A. Jaffers. [/]
[TheMariner] Pretty straight story, eh? [/]
[Narrator] Names and everything. ” “ Lord! ” said Mr. Marvel, looking nervously about him, trying to count the money in his pockets by his unaided sense of touch, and full of a strange and novel idea. [/]
[Narrator] “ It sounds most astonishing. ” “ Do n’t it? [/]
[TheMariner] Extra - ordinary, _ I _ call it. [/]
[Narrator] Never heard tell of Invisible Men before, I have n’t, but nowadays one hears such a lot of extra - ordinary things — that — ” “ That all he did? ” asked Marvel, trying to seem at his ease. [/]
[TheMariner] “ It ’s enough, ai n’t it? ” said the mariner. [/]
[MrMarvel] “ Did n’t go Back by any chance? ” asked Marvel. [/]
[Narrator] “ Just escaped and that ’s all, eh? ” “ All! ” said the mariner. [/]
[Narrator] “ Why! —ain’t it enough? ” “ Quite enough, ” said Marvel. [/]
[TheMariner] “ I should think it was enough, ” said the mariner. [/]
[Narrator] “ I should think it was enough. ” “ He did n’t have any pals — it do n’t say he had any pals, does it? ” asked Mr. Marvel, anxious. [/]
[TheMariner] “ Ai n’t one of a sort enough for you? ” asked the mariner. [/]
[TheMariner] “ No, thank Heaven, as one might say, he did n’t. ” [/]
[Narrator] He nodded his head slowly. [/]
[MrMarvel] “ It makes me regular uncomfortable, the bare thought of that chap running about the country! [/]
[MrMarvel] He is at present At Large, and from certain evidence it is supposed that he has — taken—_took _, I suppose they mean — the road to Port Stowe. [/]
[MrMarvel] You see we ’re right _ in _ it! [/]
[MrMarvel] None of your American wonders, this time. [/]
[MrMarvel] And just think of the things he might do! [/]
[MrMarvel] Where ’d you be, if he took a drop over and above, and had a fancy to go for you? [/]
[MrMarvel] Suppose he wants to rob — who can prevent him? [/]
[MrMarvel] He can trespass, he can burgle, he could walk through a cordon of policemen as easy as me or you could give the slip to a blind man! [/]
[MrMarvel] Easier! [/]
[MrMarvel] For these here blind chaps hear uncommon sharp, I ’m told. [/]
[MrMarvel] And wherever there was liquor he fancied — ” “ He ’s got a tremenjous advantage, certainly, ” said Mr. Marvel. [/]
[Narrator] “ And — well. .. ” “ You ’re right, ” said the mariner. [/]
[TheMariner] “ He _ has _. ” [/]
[Narrator] All this time Mr. Marvel had been glancing about him intently, listening for faint footfalls, trying to detect imperceptible movements. [/]
[Narrator] He seemed on the point of some great resolution. [/]
[Narrator] He coughed behind his hand. [/]
[MrMarvel] He looked about him again, listened, bent towards the mariner, and lowered his voice: “ The fact of it is — I happen — to know just a thing or two about this Invisible Man. [/]
[Narrator] From private sources. ” “ Oh! ” said the mariner, interested. [/]
[MrMarvel] “ _ You _? ” “ Yes, ” said Mr. Marvel. [/]
[Narrator] “ Me. ” “ Indeed! ” said the mariner. [/]
[Narrator] “ And may I ask — ” “ You ’ll be astonished, ” said Mr. Marvel behind his hand. [/]
[MrMarvel] “ It ’s tremenjous. ” [/]
[TheMariner] “ Indeed! ” said the mariner. [/]
[MrMarvel] “ The fact is, ” began Mr. Marvel eagerly in a confidential undertone. [/]
[Narrator] Suddenly his expression changed marvellously. [/]
[MrMarvel] “ Ow! ” he said. [/]
[Narrator] He rose stiffly in his seat. [/]
[Narrator] His face was eloquent of physical suffering. [/]
[MrMarvel] “ Wow! ” [/]
[Narrator] he said. [/]
[TheMariner] “ What ’s up? ” said the mariner, concerned. [/]
[MrMarvel] “ Toothache, ” said Mr. Marvel, and put his hand to his ear. [/]
[Narrator] He caught hold of his books. [/]
[MrMarvel] “ I must be getting on, I think, ” he said. [/]
[Narrator] He edged in a curious way along the seat away from his interlocutor. [/]
[TheMariner] “ But you was just a - going to tell me about this here Invisible Man! ” [/]
[Narrator] protested the mariner. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Marvel seemed to consult with himself. [/]
[AVoice] “ Hoax, ” said a Voice. [/]
[MrMarvel] “ It ’s a hoax, ” said Mr. Marvel. [/]
[TheMariner] “ But it ’s in the paper, ” said the mariner. [/]
[MrMarvel] “ Hoax all the same, ” said Marvel. [/]
[MrMarvel] “ I know the chap that started the lie. [/]
[MrMarvel] There ai n’t no Invisible Man whatsoever — Blimey. ” [/]
[TheMariner] “ But how ’bout this paper? [/]
[Narrator] D’you mean to say —? ” “ Not a word of it, ” said Marvel, stoutly. [/]
[Narrator] The mariner stared, paper in hand. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Marvel jerkily faced about. [/]
[Narrator] “ Wait a bit, ” said the mariner, rising and speaking slowly, “ D’you mean to say —? ” “ I do, ” said Mr. Marvel. [/]
[TheMariner] “ Then why did you let me go on and tell you all this blarsted stuff, then? [/]
[TheMariner] What d’yer mean by letting a man make a fool of himself like that for? [/]
[TheMariner] Eh? ” Mr. Marvel blew out his cheeks. [/]
[Narrator] The mariner was suddenly very red indeed; he clenched his hands. [/]
[Narrator] “ I been talking here this ten minutes, ” he said; “ and you, you little pot - bellied, leathery - faced son of an old boot, could n’t have the elementary manners — ” “ Do n’t you come bandying words with _ me _, ” said Mr. Marvel. [/]
[TheMariner] “ Bandying words! [/]
[Narrator] I ’m a jolly good mind — ” “ Come up, ” said a Voice, and Mr. Marvel was suddenly whirled about and started marching off in a curious spasmodic manner. [/]
[TheMariner] “ You ’d better move on, ” said the mariner. [/]
[MrMarvel] “ Who ’s moving on? ” said Mr. Marvel. [/]
[Narrator] He was receding obliquely with a curious hurrying gait, with occasional violent jerks forward. [/]
[Narrator] Some way along the road he began a muttered monologue, protests and recriminations. [/]
[TheMariner] “ Silly devil! ” said the mariner, legs wide apart, elbows akimbo, watching the receding figure. [/]
[TheMariner] “ I ’ll show you, you silly ass — hoaxing _ me _! [/]
[TheMariner] It ’s here — on the paper! ” [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Marvel retorted incoherently and, receding, was hidden by a bend in the road, but the mariner still stood magnificent in the midst of the way, until the approach of a butcher ’s cart dislodged him. [/]
[Narrator] Then he turned himself towards Port Stowe. [/]
[TheMariner] “ Full of extra - ordinary asses, ” he said softly to himself. [/]
[TheMariner] “ Just to take me down a bit — that was his silly game — It ’s on the paper! ” [/]
[Narrator] And there was another extraordinary thing he was presently to hear, that had happened quite close to him. [/]
[TheMariner] And that was a vision of a “ fist full of money ” (no less) travelling without visible agency, along by the wall at the corner of St. Michael ’s Lane. [/]
[Narrator] A brother mariner had seen this wonderful sight that very morning. [/]
[Narrator] He had snatched at the money forthwith and had been knocked headlong, and when he had got to his feet the butterfly money had vanished. [/]
[Narrator] Our mariner was in the mood to believe anything, he declared, but that was a bit _ too _ stiff. [/]
[Narrator] Afterwards, however, he began to think things over. [/]
[Narrator] The story of the flying money was true. [/]
[Narrator] And all about that neighbourhood, even from the august London and Country Banking Company, from the tills of shops and inns — doors standing that sunny weather entirely open — money had been quietly and dexterously making off that day in handfuls and rouleaux, floating quietly along by walls and shady places, dodging quickly from the approaching eyes of men. [/]
[Narrator] And it had, though no man had traced it, invariably ended its mysterious flight in the pocket of that agitated gentleman in the obsolete silk hat, sitting outside the little inn on the outskirts of Port Stowe. [/]
[Narrator] It was ten days after — and indeed only when the Burdock story was already old — that the mariner collated these facts and began to understand how near he had been to the wonderful Invisible Man. CHAPTER XV. [/]
[Narrator] THE MAN WHO WAS RUNNING In the early evening time Dr. Kemp was sitting in his study in the belvedere on the hill overlooking Burdock. [/]
[Narrator] It was a pleasant little room, with three windows — north, west, and south — and bookshelves covered with books and scientific publications, and a broad writing - table, and, under the north window, a microscope, glass slips, minute instruments, some cultures, and scattered bottles of reagents. [/]
[Narrator] Dr. Kemp ’s solar lamp was lit, albeit the sky was still bright with the sunset light, and his blinds were up because there was no offence of peering outsiders to require them pulled down. [/]
[Narrator] Dr. Kemp was a tall and slender young man, with flaxen hair and a moustache almost white, and the work he was upon would earn him, he hoped, the fellowship of the Royal Society, so highly did he think of it. [/]
[Narrator] And his eye, presently wandering from his work, caught the sunset blazing at the back of the hill that is over against his own. [/]
[Narrator] For a minute perhaps he sat, pen in mouth, admiring the rich golden colour above the crest, and then his attention was attracted by the little figure of a man, inky black, running over the hill - brow towards him. [/]
[Narrator] He was a shortish little man, and he wore a high hat, and he was running so fast that his legs verily twinkled. [/]
[Kemp] “ Another of those fools, ” said Dr. Kemp. [/]
[Kemp] “ Like that ass who ran into me this morning round a corner, with the ‘ ’ Visible Man a - coming, sir! ’ [/]
[Kemp] I ca n’t imagine what possesses people. [/]
[Kemp] One might think we were in the thirteenth century. ” [/]
[Narrator] He got up, went to the window, and stared at the dusky hillside, and the dark little figure tearing down it. [/]
[Kemp] “ He seems in a confounded hurry, ” said Dr. Kemp, “ but he does n’t seem to be getting on. [/]
[Kemp] If his pockets were full of lead, he could n’t run heavier. ” “ Spurted, sir, ” said Dr. Kemp. [/]
[Narrator] In another moment the higher of the villas that had clambered up the hill from Burdock had occulted the running figure. [/]
[Narrator] He was visible again for a moment, and again, and then again, three times between the three detached houses that came next, and then the terrace hid him. [/]
[Kemp] “ Asses! ” said Dr. Kemp, swinging round on his heel and walking back to his writing - table. [/]
[Narrator] But those who saw the fugitive nearer, and perceived the abject terror on his perspiring face, being themselves in the open roadway, did not share in the doctor ’s contempt. [/]
[Narrator] By the man pounded, and as he ran he chinked like a well - filled purse that is tossed to and fro. [/]
[Narrator] He looked neither to the right nor the left, but his dilated eyes stared straight downhill to where the lamps were being lit, and the people were crowded in the street. [/]
[Narrator] And his ill - shaped mouth fell apart, and a glairy foam lay on his lips, and his breath came hoarse and noisy. [/]
[Narrator] All he passed stopped and began staring up the road and down, and interrogating one another with an inkling of discomfort for the reason of his haste. [/]
[Narrator] And then presently, far up the hill, a dog playing in the road yelped and ran under a gate, and as they still wondered something — a wind — a pad, pad, pad, —a sound like a panting breathing, rushed by. [/]
[Narrator] People screamed. [/]
[Narrator] People sprang off the pavement: It passed in shouts, it passed by instinct down the hill. [/]
[Narrator] They were shouting in the street before Marvel was halfway there. [/]
[Narrator] They were bolting into houses and slamming the doors behind them, with the news. [/]
[Narrator] He heard it and made one last desperate spurt. [/]
[Narrator] Fear came striding by, rushed ahead of him, and in a moment had seized the town. [/]
[Fear] “ The Invisible Man is coming! [/]
[Fear] The Invisible Man! ” CHAPTER XVI. [/]
[ABlackBeardedManInGrey] IN THE “ JOLLY CRICKETERS ” The “ Jolly Cricketers ” is just at the bottom of the hill, where the tram - lines begin. [/]

[Narrator] After five minutes, during which his mind had travelled into a remote speculation of social conditions of the future, and lost itself at last over the time dimension, Dr. Kemp roused himself with a sigh, pulled down the window again, and returned to his writing desk. [/]

[Kemp] CERTAIN FIRST PRINCIPLES “ What ’s the matter? ” asked Kemp, when the Invisible Man admitted him. [/]

[Kemp] “ But, confound it! [/]

[TheInvisibleMan] I gather it was a secret. [/]

[Kemp] “ Before we can do anything else, ” said Kemp, “ I must understand a little more about this invisibility of yours. ” [/]
[Narrator] He had sat down, after one nervous glance out of the window, with the air of a man who has talking to do. [/]
[Narrator] His doubts of the sanity of the entire business flashed and vanished again as he looked across to where Griffin sat at the breakfast - table — a headless, handless dressing - gown, wiping unseen lips on a miraculously held serviette. [/]
[Griffin] “ It ’s simple enough — and credible enough, ” said Griffin, putting the serviette aside and leaning the invisible head on an invisible hand. [/]
[Kemp] “ No doubt, to you, but — ” Kemp laughed. [/]
[Kemp] “ Well, yes; to me it seemed wonderful at first, no doubt. [/]
[Kemp] But now, great God! .. . [/]
[Kemp] But we will do great things yet! [/]
[Kemp] I came on the stuff first at Chesilstowe. ” “ Chesilstowe? ” “ I went there after I left London. [/]
[Kemp] You know I dropped medicine and took up physics? [/]
[Kemp] No; well, I did. [/]
[Kemp] _ [/]
[Kemp] Light _ fascinated me. ” [/]
[Kemp] “ Ah! ” “ Optical density! [/]
[Kemp] The whole subject is a network of riddles — a network with solutions glimmering elusively through. [/]
[Kemp] And being but two - and - twenty and full of enthusiasm, I said, ‘ I will devote my life to this. [/]
[Kemp] This is worth while. ’ [/]
[Kemp] You know what fools we are at two - and - twenty? ” “ Fools then or fools now, ” said Kemp. [/]
[Kemp] “ As though knowing could be any satisfaction to a man! [/]
[Kemp] “ But I went to work — like a slave. [/]
[Kemp] And I had hardly worked and thought about the matter six months before light came through one of the meshes suddenly — blindingly! [/]
[Kemp] I found a general principle of pigments and refraction — a formula, a geometrical expression involving four dimensions. [/]
[Kemp] Fools, common men, even common mathematicians, do not know anything of what some general expression may mean to the student of molecular physics. [/]
[Kemp] In the books — the books that tramp has hidden — there are marvels, miracles! [/]
[Kemp] But this was not a method, it was an idea, that might lead to a method by which it would be possible, without changing any other property of matter — except, in some instances colours — to lower the refractive index of a substance, solid or liquid, to that of air — so far as all practical purposes are concerned. ” [/]
[Kemp] “ Phew! ” said Kemp. [/]
[Kemp] “ That ’s odd! [/]
[Kemp] But still I do n’t see quite. .. I can understand that thereby you could spoil a valuable stone, but personal invisibility is a far cry. ” [/]
[Griffin] “ Precisely, ” said Griffin. [/]
[Griffin] “ But consider, visibility depends on the action of the visible bodies on light. [/]
[Griffin] Either a body absorbs light, or it reflects or refracts it, or does all these things. [/]
[Griffin] If it neither reflects nor refracts nor absorbs light, it can not of itself be visible. [/]
[Griffin] You see an opaque red box, for instance, because the colour absorbs some of the light and reflects the rest, all the red part of the light, to you. [/]
[Griffin] If it did not absorb any particular part of the light, but reflected it all, then it would be a shining white box. [/]
[Griffin] Silver! [/]
[Griffin] A diamond box would neither absorb much of the light nor reflect much from the general surface, but just here and there where the surfaces were favourable the light would be reflected and refracted, so that you would get a brilliant appearance of flashing reflections and translucencies — a sort of skeleton of light. [/]
[Griffin] A glass box would not be so brilliant, nor so clearly visible, as a diamond box, because there would be less refraction and reflection. [/]
[Griffin] See that? [/]
[Griffin] From certain points of view you would see quite clearly through it. [/]
[Griffin] Some kinds of glass would be more visible than others, a box of flint glass would be brighter than a box of ordinary window glass. [/]
[Griffin] A box of very thin common glass would be hard to see in a bad light, because it would absorb hardly any light and refract and reflect very little. [/]
[Griffin] And if you put a sheet of common white glass in water, still more if you put it in some denser liquid than water, it would vanish almost altogether, because light passing from water to glass is only slightly refracted or reflected or indeed affected in any way. [/]
[Griffin] It is almost as invisible as a jet of coal gas or hydrogen is in air. [/]
[Narrator] And for precisely the same reason! ” “ Yes, ” said Kemp, “ that is pretty plain sailing. ” [/]
[Griffin] “ And here is another fact you will know to be true. [/]
[Griffin] If a sheet of glass is smashed, Kemp, and beaten into a powder, it becomes much more visible while it is in the air; it becomes at last an opaque white powder. [/]
[Griffin] This is because the powdering multiplies the surfaces of the glass at which refraction and reflection occur. [/]
[Griffin] In the sheet of glass there are only two surfaces; in the powder the light is reflected or refracted by each grain it passes through, and very little gets right through the powder. [/]
[Griffin] But if the white powdered glass is put into water, it forthwith vanishes. [/]
[Griffin] The powdered glass and water have much the same refractive index; that is, the light undergoes very little refraction or reflection in passing from one to the other. [/]
[Kemp] “ You make the glass invisible by putting it into a liquid of nearly the same refractive index; a transparent thing becomes invisible if it is put in any medium of almost the same refractive index. [/]
[Kemp] And if you will consider only a second, you will see also that the powder of glass might be made to vanish in air, if its refractive index could be made the same as that of air; for then there would be no refraction or reflection as the light passed from glass to air. ” [/]
[Kemp] “ Yes, yes, ” said Kemp. [/]
[Narrator] “ But a man ’s not powdered glass! ” “ No, ” said Griffin. [/]
[Narrator] “ He ’s more transparent! ” “ Nonsense! ” “ That from a doctor! [/]
[Griffin] How one forgets! [/]
[Griffin] Have you already forgotten your physics, in ten years? [/]
[Griffin] Just think of all the things that are transparent and seem not to be so. [/]
[Griffin] Paper, for instance, is made up of transparent fibres, and it is white and opaque only for the same reason that a powder of glass is white and opaque. [/]
[Griffin] Oil white paper, fill up the interstices between the particles with oil so that there is no longer refraction or reflection except at the surfaces, and it becomes as transparent as glass. [/]
[Griffin] And not only paper, but cotton fibre, linen fibre, wool fibre, woody fibre, and _ bone _, Kemp, _ flesh _, Kemp, _ hair _, Kemp, _ nails _ and _ nerves _, Kemp, in fact the whole fabric of a man except the red of his blood and the black pigment of hair, are all made up of transparent, colourless tissue. [/]
[Griffin] So little suffices to make us visible one to the other. [/]
[Griffin] For the most part the fibres of a living creature are no more opaque than water. ” [/]
[Kemp] “ Great Heavens! ” cried Kemp. [/]
[Kemp] “ Of course, of course! [/]
[Narrator] I was thinking only last night of the sea larvae and all jelly - fish! ” “ _ Now _ [/]
[Griffin] you have me! [/]
[Griffin] And all that I knew and had in mind a year after I left London — six years ago. [/]
[Griffin] But I kept it to myself. [/]
[Griffin] I had to do my work under frightful disadvantages. [/]
[Griffin] Oliver, my professor, was a scientific bounder, a journalist by instinct, a thief of ideas — he was always prying! [/]

[Narrator] AT THE HOUSE IN GREAT PORTLAND STREET For a moment Kemp sat in silence, staring at the back of the headless figure at the window. [/]
[Narrator] Then he started, struck by a thought, rose, took the Invisible Man ’s arm, and turned him away from the outlook. [/]

[Griffin] For a space Griffin sat silent, and then he resumed abruptly: “ I had left the Chesilstowe cottage already, ” he said, “ when that happened. [/]
[Griffin] It was last December. [/]
[Griffin] I had taken a room in London, a large unfurnished room in a big ill - managed lodging - house in a slum near Great Portland Street. [/]
[Griffin] The room was soon full of the appliances I had bought with his money; the work was going on steadily, successfully, drawing near an end. [/]
[Griffin] I was like a man emerging from a thicket, and suddenly coming on some unmeaning tragedy. [/]
[Griffin] I went to bury him. [/]
[Griffin] My mind was still on this research, and I did not lift a finger to save his character. [/]
[Griffin] I remember the funeral, the cheap hearse, the scant ceremony, the windy frost - bitten hillside, and the old college friend of his who read the service over him — a shabby, black, bent old man with a snivelling cold. [/]
[Griffin] “ I remember walking back to the empty house, through the place that had once been a village and was now patched and tinkered by the jerry builders into the ugly likeness of a town. [/]
[Griffin] Every way the roads ran out at last into the desecrated fields and ended in rubble heaps and rank wet weeds. [/]
[Griffin] I remember myself as a gaunt black figure, going along the slippery, shiny pavement, and the strange sense of detachment I felt from the squalid respectability, the sordid commercialism of the place. [/]
[Griffin] “ I did not feel a bit sorry for my father. [/]
[Griffin] He seemed to me to be the victim of his own foolish sentimentality. [/]
[Griffin] The current ca nt required my attendance at his funeral, but it was really not my affair. [/]
[Griffin] “ But going along the High Street, my old life came back to me for a space, for I met the girl I had known ten years since. [/]
[Griffin] Our eyes met. [/]
[Griffin] “ Something moved me to turn back and talk to her. [/]
[Griffin] She was a very ordinary person. [/]
[Griffin] “ It was all like a dream, that visit to the old places. [/]
[Griffin] I did not feel then that I was lonely, that I had come out from the world into a desolate place. [/]
[Griffin] I appreciated my loss of sympathy, but I put it down to the general inanity of things. [/]
[Griffin] Re - entering my room seemed like the recovery of reality. [/]
[Griffin] There were the things I knew and loved. [/]
[Griffin] There stood the apparatus, the experiments arranged and waiting. [/]
[Griffin] And now there was scarcely a difficulty left, beyond the planning of details. [/]
[Griffin] “ I will tell you, Kemp, sooner or later, all the complicated processes. [/]

[Griffin] We must hunt him down. [/]
[Griffin] We must get those books again. [/]
[Griffin] But the essential phase was to place the transparent object whose refractive index was to be lowered between two radiating centres of a sort of ethereal vibration, of which I will tell you more fully later. [/]
[Griffin] No, not those Röntgen vibrations — I do n’t know that these others of mine have been described. [/]
[Griffin] Yet they are obvious enough. [/]
[Griffin] I needed two little dynamos, and these I worked with a cheap gas engine. [/]
[Griffin] My first experiment was with a bit of white wool fabric. [/]
[Griffin] It was the strangest thing in the world to see it in the flicker of the flashes soft and white, and then to watch it fade like a wreath of smoke and vanish. [/]
[Griffin] “ I could scarcely believe I had done it. [/]
[Griffin] I put my hand into the emptiness, and there was the thing as solid as ever. [/]
[Griffin] I felt it awkwardly, and threw it on the floor. [/]
[Griffin] I had a little trouble finding it again. [/]
[Griffin] “ And then came a curious experience. [/]
[Griffin] I heard a miaow behind me, and turning, saw a lean white cat, very dirty, on the cistern cover outside the window. [/]
[Griffin] A thought came into my head. [/]
[Griffin] ‘ Everything ready for you, ’ I said, and went to the window, opened it, and called softly. [/]
[Griffin] She came in, purring — the poor beast was starving — and I gave her some milk. [/]
[Griffin] All my food was in a cupboard in the corner of the room. [/]
[Griffin] After that she went smelling round the room, evidently with the idea of making herself at home. [/]
[Griffin] The invisible rag upset her a bit; you should have seen her spit at it! [/]
[Griffin] But I made her comfortable on the pillow of my truckle - bed. [/]
[Griffin] And I gave her butter to get her to wash. ” [/]
[Griffin] “ And you processed her? ” “ I processed her. [/]
[Griffin] But giving drugs to a cat is no joke, Kemp! [/]
[Griffin] And the process failed. ” “ Failed! ” [/]
[Griffin] “ In two particulars. [/]
[Griffin] These were the claws and the pigment stuff, what is it? —at the back of the eye in a cat. [/]
[Griffin] You know? ” “ _ Tapetum _. ” [/]
[Griffin] “ Yes, the _ tapetum _. [/]
[Griffin] It did n’t go. [/]
[Griffin] After I ’d given the stuff to bleach the blood and done certain other things to her, I gave the beast opium, and put her and the pillow she was sleeping on, on the apparatus. [/]
[Griffin] And after all the rest had faded and vanished, there remained two little ghosts of her eyes. ” “ Odd! ” “ I ca n’t explain it. [/]
[Griffin] She was bandaged and clamped, of course — so I had her safe; but she woke while she was still misty, and miaowed dismally, and someone came knocking. [/]
[Griffin] It was an old woman from downstairs, who suspected me of vivisecting — a drink - sodden old creature, with only a white cat to care for in all the world. [/]
[Griffin] I whipped out some chloroform, applied it, and answered the door. [/]
[Griffin] ‘ Did I hear a cat? ’ [/]
[Griffin] she asked. [/]
[Griffin] ‘ My cat? ’ [/]
[Griffin] ‘ Not here, ’ said I, very politely. [/]
[Griffin] She was a little doubtful and tried to peer past me into the room; strange enough to her no doubt — bare walls, uncurtained windows, truckle - bed, with the gas engine vibrating, and the seethe of the radiant points, and that faint ghastly stinging of chloroform in the air. [/]
[Griffin] She had to be satisfied at last and went away again. ” [/]
[Kemp] “ How long did it take? ” [/]
[Narrator] asked Kemp. [/]
[Griffin] “ Three or four hours — the cat. [/]
[Griffin] The bones and sinews and the fat were the last to go, and the tips of the coloured hairs. [/]
[Griffin] And, as I say, the back part of the eye, tough, iridescent stuff it is, would n’t go at all. [/]
[Griffin] “ It was night outside long before the business was over, and nothing was to be seen but the dim eyes and the claws. [/]
[Griffin] I stopped the gas engine, felt for and stroked the beast, which was still insensible, and then, being tired, left it sleeping on the invisible pillow and went to bed. [/]
[Griffin] I found it hard to sleep. [/]
[Griffin] I lay awake thinking weak aimless stuff, going over the experiment over and over again, or dreaming feverishly of things growing misty and vanishing about me, until everything, the ground I stood on, vanished, and so I came to that sickly falling nightmare one gets. [/]
[Griffin] About two, the cat began miaowing about the room. [/]
[Griffin] I tried to hush it by talking to it, and then I decided to turn it out. [/]
[Griffin] I remember the shock I had when striking a light — there were just the round eyes shining green — and nothing round them. [/]
[Griffin] I would have given it milk, but I had n’t any. [/]
[Griffin] It would n’t be quiet, it just sat down and miaowed at the door. [/]
[Griffin] I tried to catch it, with an idea of putting it out of the window, but it would n’t be caught, it vanished. [/]

[Griffin] I found sleeping was hopeless, and, locking my door after me, wandered out into the morning streets. ” [/]
[Kemp] “ You do n’t mean to say there ’s an invisible cat at large! ” said Kemp. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ If it has n’t been killed, ” said the Invisible Man. [/]
[Narrator] “ Why not? ” “ Why not? ” said Kemp. [/]
[Kemp] “ I did n’t mean to interrupt. ” [/]

[Narrator] He was silent for the best part of a minute. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Then he resumed abruptly: “ I remember that morning before the change very vividly. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I must have gone up Great Portland Street. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I remember the barracks in Albany Street, and the horse soldiers coming out, and at last I found the summit of Primrose Hill. [/]
