[TheInvisibleMan] My weary brain tried to formulate the position, to plot out a plan of action. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ I was surprised to find, now that my prize was within my grasp, how inconclusive its attainment seemed. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] As a matter of fact I was worked out; the intense stress of nearly four years ’ continuous work left me incapable of any strength of feeling. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I was apathetic, and I tried in vain to recover the enthusiasm of my first inquiries, the passion of discovery that had enabled me to compass even the downfall of my father ’s grey hairs. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Nothing seemed to matter. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I saw pretty clearly this was a transient mood, due to overwork and want of sleep, and that either by drugs or rest it would be possible to recover my energies. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ All I could think clearly was that the thing had to be carried through; the fixed idea still ruled me. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] And soon, for the money I had was almost exhausted. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I looked about me at the hillside, with children playing and girls watching them, and tried to think of all the fantastic advantages an invisible man would have in the world. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] After a time I crawled home, took some food and a strong dose of strychnine, and went to sleep in my clothes on my unmade bed. [/]
[Narrator] Strychnine is a grand tonic, Kemp, to take the flabbiness out of a man. ” “ It ’s the devil, ” said Kemp. [/]
[Narrator] “ It ’s the palaeolithic in a bottle. ” “ I awoke vastly invigorated and rather irritable. [/]
[Narrator] You know? ” “ I know the stuff. ” [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ And there was someone rapping at the door. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] It was my landlord with threats and inquiries, an old Polish Jew in a long grey coat and greasy slippers. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I had been tormenting a cat in the night, he was sure — the old woman ’s tongue had been busy. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] He insisted on knowing all about it. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] The laws in this country against vivisection were very severe — he might be liable. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I denied the cat. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Then the vibration of the little gas engine could be felt all over the house, he said. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] That was true, certainly. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] He edged round me into the room, peering about over his German - silver spectacles, and a sudden dread came into my mind that he might carry away something of my secret. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I tried to keep between him and the concentrating apparatus I had arranged, and that only made him more curious. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] What was I doing? [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Why was I always alone and secretive? [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Was it legal? [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Was it dangerous? [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I paid nothing but the usual rent. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] His had always been a most respectable house — in a disreputable neighbourhood. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Suddenly my temper gave way. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I told him to get out. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] He began to protest, to jabber of his right of entry. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] In a moment I had him by the collar; something ripped, and he went spinning out into his own passage. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I slammed and locked the door and sat down quivering. [/]
[Kemp] “ He made a fuss outside, which I disregarded, and after a time he went away. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ But this brought matters to a crisis. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I did not know what he would do, nor even what he had the power to do. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] To move to fresh apartments would have meant delay; altogether I had barely twenty pounds left in the world, for the most part in a bank — and I could not afford that. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Vanish! [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] It was irresistible. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Then there would be an inquiry, the sacking of my room. [/]
[Kemp] “ At the thought of the possibility of my work being exposed or interrupted at its very climax, I became very angry and active. [/]
[Kemp] I hurried out with my three books of notes, my cheque - book — the tramp has them now — and directed them from the nearest Post Office to a house of call for letters and parcels in Great Portland Street. [/]
[Kemp] I tried to go out noiselessly. [/]
[Kemp] Coming in, I found my landlord going quietly upstairs; he had heard the door close, I suppose. [/]
[Kemp] You would have laughed to see him jump aside on the landing as I came tearing after him. [/]
[Kemp] He glared at me as I went by him, and I made the house quiver with the slamming of my door. [/]
[Kemp] I heard him come shuffling up to my floor, hesitate, and go down. [/]
[Kemp] I set to work upon my preparations forthwith. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ It was all done that evening and night. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] While I was still sitting under the sickly, drowsy influence of the drugs that decolourise blood, there came a repeated knocking at the door. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] It ceased, footsteps went away and returned, and the knocking was resumed. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] There was an attempt to push something under the door — a blue paper. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Then in a fit of irritation I rose and went and flung the door wide open. [/]
[Narrator] ‘ Now then? ’ said I. “ It was my landlord, with a notice of ejectment or something. [/]
[Kemp] He held it out to me, saw something odd about my hands, I expect, and lifted his eyes to my face. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ For a moment he gaped. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Then he gave a sort of inarticulate cry, dropped candle and writ together, and went blundering down the dark passage to the stairs. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I shut the door, locked it, and went to the looking - glass. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Then I understood his terror. .. . [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] My face was white — like white stone. [/]
[Kemp] “ But it was all horrible. [/]
[Kemp] I had not expected the suffering. [/]
[Kemp] A night of racking anguish, sickness and fainting. [/]
[Kemp] I set my teeth, though my skin was presently afire, all my body afire; but I lay there like grim death. [/]
[Kemp] I understood now how it was the cat had howled until I chloroformed it. [/]
[Kemp] Lucky it was I lived alone and untended in my room. [/]
[Kemp] There were times when I sobbed and groaned and talked. [/]
[Kemp] But I stuck to it. .. . [/]
[Kemp] I became insensible and woke languid in the darkness. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ The pain had passed. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I thought I was killing myself and I did not care. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I shall never forget that dawn, and the strange horror of seeing that my hands had become as clouded glass, and watching them grow clearer and thinner as the day went by, until at last I could see the sickly disorder of my room through them, though I closed my transparent eyelids. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] My limbs became glassy, the bones and arteries faded, vanished, and the little white nerves went last. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I gritted my teeth and stayed there to the end. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] At last only the dead tips of the fingernails remained, pallid and white, and the brown stain of some acid upon my fingers. [/]
[Kemp] “ I struggled up. [/]
[Kemp] At first I was as incapable as a swathed infant — stepping with limbs I could not see. [/]
[Kemp] I was weak and very hungry. [/]
[Kemp] I went and stared at nothing in my shaving - glass, at nothing save where an attenuated pigment still remained behind the retina of my eyes, fainter than mist. [/]
[Kemp] I had to hang on to the table and press my forehead against the glass. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ It was only by a frantic effort of will that I dragged myself back to the apparatus and completed the process. [/]
[Kemp] “ I slept during the forenoon, pulling the sheet over my eyes to shut out the light, and about midday I was awakened again by a knocking. [/]
[Kemp] My strength had returned. [/]
[Kemp] I sat up and listened and heard a whispering. [/]
[Kemp] I sprang to my feet and as noiselessly as possible began to detach the connections of my apparatus, and to distribute it about the room, so as to destroy the suggestions of its arrangement. [/]
[Kemp] Presently the knocking was renewed and voices called, first my landlord ’s, and then two others. [/]
[Kemp] To gain time I answered them. [/]
[Kemp] The invisible rag and pillow came to hand [/]
[Kemp] and I opened the window and pitched them out on to the cistern cover. [/]
[Kemp] As the window opened, a heavy crash came at the door. [/]
[Kemp] Someone had charged it with the idea of smashing the lock. [/]
[Kemp] But the stout bolts I had screwed up some days before stopped him. [/]
[Kemp] That startled me, made me angry. [/]
[Kemp] I began to tremble and do things hurriedly. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ I tossed together some loose paper, straw, packing paper and so forth, in the middle of the room, and turned on the gas. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Heavy blows began to rain upon the door. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I could not find the matches. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I beat my hands on the wall with rage. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I turned down the gas again, stepped out of the window on the cistern cover, very softly lowered the sash, and sat down, secure and invisible, but quivering with anger, to watch events. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] They split a panel, I saw, and in another moment they had broken away the staples of the bolts and stood in the open doorway. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] It was the landlord and his two step - sons, sturdy young men of three or four and twenty. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Behind them fluttered the old hag of a woman from downstairs. [/]
[Kemp] “ You may imagine their astonishment to find the room empty. [/]
[Kemp] One of the younger men rushed to the window at once, flung it up and stared out. [/]
[Kemp] His staring eyes and thick - lipped bearded face came a foot from my face. [/]
[Kemp] I was half minded to hit his silly countenance, but I arrested my doubled fist. [/]
[Kemp] He stared right through me. [/]
[Kemp] So did the others as they joined him. [/]
[Kemp] The old man went and peered under the bed, and then they all made a rush for the cupboard. [/]
[Kemp] They had to argue about it at length in Yiddish and Cockney English. [/]
[Kemp] They concluded I had not answered them, that their imagination had deceived them. [/]
[Kemp] A feeling of extraordinary elation took the place of my anger as I sat outside the window and watched these four people — for the old lady came in, glancing suspiciously about her like a cat, trying to understand the riddle of my behaviour. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ The old man, so far as I could understand his _ patois _, agreed with the old lady that I was a vivisectionist. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] The sons protested in garbled English that I was an electrician, and appealed to the dynamos and radiators. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] They were all nervous about my arrival, although I found subsequently that they had bolted the front door. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] The old lady peered into the cupboard and under the bed, and one of the young men pushed up the register and stared up the chimney. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] One of my fellow lodgers, a coster - monger who shared the opposite room with a butcher, appeared on the landing, and he was called in and told incoherent things. [/]
[Kemp] “ It occurred to me that the radiators, if they fell into the hands of some acute well - educated person, would give me away too much, and watching my opportunity, I came into the room and tilted one of the little dynamos off its fellow on which it was standing, and smashed both apparatus. [/]
[Kemp] Then, while they were trying to explain the smash, I dodged out of the room and went softly downstairs. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ I went into one of the sitting - rooms and waited until they came down, still speculating and argumentative, all a little disappointed at finding no ‘ horrors, ’ and all a little puzzled how they stood legally towards me. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Then I slipped up again with a box of matches, fired my heap of paper and rubbish, put the chairs and bedding thereby, led the gas to the affair, by means of an india - rubber tube, and waving a farewell to the room left it for the last time. ” [/]
[Kemp] “ You fired the house! ” [/]
[Narrator] exclaimed Kemp. [/]
[Kemp] “ Fired the house. [/]
[Kemp] It was the only way to cover my trail — and no doubt it was insured. [/]
[Kemp] I slipped the bolts of the front door quietly and went out into the street. [/]
[Kemp] I was invisible, and I was only just beginning to realise the extraordinary advantage my invisibility gave me. [/]
[Kemp] My head was already teeming with plans of all the wild and wonderful things I had now impunity to do. ” CHAPTER XXI. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] IN OXFORD STREET “ In going downstairs the first time I found an unexpected difficulty because I could not see my feet; indeed I stumbled twice, and there was an unaccustomed clumsiness in gripping the bolt. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] By not looking down, however, I managed to walk on the level passably well. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ My mood, I say, was one of exaltation. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I felt as a seeing man might do, with padded feet and noiseless clothes, in a city of the blind. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I experienced a wild impulse to jest, to startle people, to clap men on the back, fling people ’s hats astray, and generally revel in my extraordinary advantage. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ But hardly had I emerged upon Great Portland Street, however (my lodging was close to the big draper ’s shop there), when I heard a clashing concussion and was hit violently behind, and turning saw a man carrying a basket of soda - water syphons, and looking in amazement at his burden. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Although the blow had really hurt me, I found something so irresistible in his astonishment that I laughed aloud. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] ‘ The devil ’s in the basket, ’ I said, and suddenly twisted it out of his hand. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] He let go incontinently, and I swung the whole weight into the air. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ But a fool of a cabman, standing outside a public house, made a sudden rush for this, and his extending fingers took me with excruciating violence under the ear. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I let the whole down with a smash on the cabman, and then, with shouts and the clatter of feet about me, people coming out of shops, vehicles pulling up, I realised what I had done for myself, and cursing my folly, backed against a shop window and prepared to dodge out of the confusion. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] In a moment I should be wedged into a crowd and inevitably discovered. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I pushed by a butcher boy, who luckily did not turn to see the nothingness that shoved him aside, and dodged behind the cab - man ’s four - wheeler. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I do not know how they settled the business. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I hurried straight across the road, which was happily clear, and hardly heeding which way I went, in the fright of detection the incident had given me, plunged into the afternoon throng of Oxford Street. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ I tried to get into the stream of people, but they were too thick for me, and in a moment my heels were being trodden upon. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I took to the gutter, the roughness of which I found painful to my feet, and forthwith the shaft of a crawling hansom dug me forcibly under the shoulder blade, reminding me that I was already bruised severely. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I staggered out of the way of the cab, avoided a perambulator by a convulsive movement, and found myself behind the hansom. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] A happy thought saved me, and as this drove slowly along I followed in its immediate wake, trembling and astonished at the turn of my adventure. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] And not only trembling, but shivering. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] It was a bright day in January and I was stark naked and the thin slime of mud that covered the road was freezing. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Foolish as it seems to me now, I had not reckoned that, transparent or not, I was still amenable to the weather and all its consequences. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ Then suddenly a bright idea came into my head. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I ran round and got into the cab. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] And so, shivering, scared, and sniffing with the first intimations of a cold, and with the bruises in the small of my back growing upon my attention, I drove slowly along Oxford Street and past Tottenham Court Road. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] My mood was as different from that in which I had sallied forth ten minutes ago as it is possible to imagine. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] This invisibility indeed! [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] The one thought that possessed me was — how was I to get out of the scrape I was in. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ We crawled past Mudie ’s, and there a tall woman with five or six yellow - labelled books hailed my cab, and I sprang out just in time to escape her, shaving a railway van narrowly in my flight. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I made off up the roadway to Bloomsbury Square, intending to strike north past the Museum and so get into the quiet district. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I was now cruelly chilled, and the strangeness of my situation so unnerved me that I whimpered as I ran. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] At the northward corner of the Square a little white dog ran out of the Pharmaceutical Society ’s offices, and incontinently made for me, nose down. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ I had never realised it before, but the nose is to the mind of a dog what the eye is to the mind of a seeing man. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Dogs perceive the scent of a man moving as men perceive his vision. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] This brute began barking and leaping, showing, as it seemed to me, only too plainly that he was aware of me. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I crossed Great Russell Street, glancing over my shoulder as I did so, and went some way along Montague Street before I realised what I was running towards. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ Then I became aware of a blare of music, and looking along the street saw a number of people advancing out of Russell Square, red shirts, and the banner of the Salvation Army to the fore. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Such a crowd, chanting in the roadway and scoffing on the pavement, I could not hope to penetrate, and dreading to go back and farther from home again, and deciding on the spur of the moment, I ran up the white steps of a house facing the museum railings, and stood there until the crowd should have passed. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Happily the dog stopped at the noise of the band too, hesitated, and turned tail, running back to Bloomsbury Square again. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ On came the band, bawling with unconscious irony some hymn about ‘ When shall we see His face? ’ [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] and it seemed an interminable time to me before the tide of the crowd washed along the pavement by me. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Thud, thud, thud, came the drum with a vibrating resonance, and for the moment I did not notice two urchins stopping at the railings by me. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] ‘ See ’em, ’ said one. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] ‘ See what? ’ said the other. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] ‘ Why — them footmarks — bare. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Like what you makes in mud. ’ “ I looked down and saw the youngsters had stopped and were gaping at the muddy footmarks I had left behind me up the newly whitened steps. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] The passing people elbowed and jostled them, but their confounded intelligence was arrested. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] ‘ Thud, thud, thud, when, thud, shall we see, thud, his face, thud, thud. ’ [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] ‘ There ’s a barefoot man gone up them steps, or I do n’t know nothing, ’ said one. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] ‘ And he ai n’t never come down again. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] And his foot was a - bleeding. ’ [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ The thick of the crowd had already passed. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] ‘ Looky there, Ted, ’ quoth the younger of the detectives, with the sharpness of surprise in his voice, and pointed straight to my feet. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I looked down and saw at once the dim suggestion of their outline sketched in splashes of mud. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] For a moment I was paralysed. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ ‘ Why, that ’s rum, ’ said the elder. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] ‘ Dashed rum! [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] It ’s just like the ghost of a foot, ai n’t it? ’ [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] He hesitated and advanced with outstretched hand. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] A man pulled up short to see what he was catching, and then a girl. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] In another moment he would have touched me. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Then I saw what to do. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I made a step, the boy started back with an exclamation, and with a rapid movement I swung myself over into the portico of the next house. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] But the smaller boy was sharp - eyed enough to follow the movement, and before I was well down the steps and upon the pavement, he had recovered from his momentary astonishment and was shouting out that the feet had gone over the wall. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ They rushed round and saw my new footmarks flash into being on the lower step and upon the pavement. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] ‘ What ’s up? ’ asked someone. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] ‘ Feet! Look! [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Feet running! ’ [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ Everybody in the road, except my three pursuers, was pouring along after the Salvation Army, and this blow not only impeded me but them. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] There was an eddy of surprise and interrogation. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] At the cost of bowling over one young fellow I got through, and in another moment I was rushing headlong round the circuit of Russell Square, with six or seven astonished people following my footmarks. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] There was no time for explanation, or else the whole host would have been after me. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ Twice I doubled round corners, thrice I crossed the road and came back upon my tracks, and then, as my feet grew hot and dry, the damp impressions began to fade. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] At last I had a breathing space and rubbed my feet clean with my hands, and so got away altogether. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] The last I saw of the chase was a little group of a dozen people perhaps, studying with infinite perplexity a slowly drying footprint that had resulted from a puddle in Tavistock Square, a footprint as isolated and incomprehensible to them as Crusoe ’s solitary discovery. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ This running warmed me to a certain extent, and I went on with a better courage through the maze of less frequented roads that runs hereabouts. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] My back had now become very stiff and sore, my tonsils were painful from the cabman ’s fingers, and the skin of my neck had been scratched by his nails; my feet hurt exceedingly and I was lame from a little cut on one foot. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I saw in time a blind man approaching me, and fled limping, for I feared his subtle intuitions. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Once or twice accidental collisions occurred and I left people amazed, with unaccountable curses ringing in their ears. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Then came something silent and quiet against my face, and across the Square fell a thin veil of slowly falling flakes of snow. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I had caught a cold, and do as I would I could not avoid an occasional sneeze. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] And every dog that came in sight, with its pointing nose and curious sniffing, was a terror to me. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ Then came men and boys running, first one and then others, and shouting as they ran. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] It was a fire. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] They ran in the direction of my lodging, and looking back down a street I saw a mass of black smoke streaming up above the roofs and telephone wires. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] It was my lodging burning; my clothes, my apparatus, all my resources indeed, except my cheque - book and the three volumes of memoranda that awaited me in Great Portland Street, were there. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Burning! [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I had burnt my boats — if ever a man did! [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] The place was blazing. ” [/]
[Narrator] The Invisible Man paused and thought. [/]
[Narrator] Kemp glanced nervously out of the window. [/]
[Kemp] “ Yes? ” he said. [/]
[Kemp] “ Go on. ” CHAPTER XXII. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] IN THE EMPORIUM “ So last January, with the beginning of a snowstorm in the air about me — and if it settled on me it would betray me! —weary, cold, painful, inexpressibly wretched, and still but half convinced of my invisible quality, I began this new life to which I am committed. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I had no refuge, no appliances, no human being in the world in whom I could confide. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] To have told my secret would have given me away — made a mere show and rarity of me. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Nevertheless, I was half - minded to accost some passer - by and throw myself upon his mercy. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] But I knew too clearly the terror and brutal cruelty my advances would evoke. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I made no plans in the street. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] My sole object was to get shelter from the snow, to get myself covered and warm; then I might hope to plan. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] But even to me, an Invisible Man, the rows of London houses stood latched, barred, and bolted impregnably. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ Only one thing could I see clearly before me — the cold exposure and misery of the snowstorm and the night. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ And then I had a brilliant idea. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I turned down one of the roads leading from Gower Street to Tottenham Court Road, and found myself outside Omniums, the big establishment where everything is to be bought — you know the place: meat, grocery, linen, furniture, clothing, oil paintings even — a huge meandering collection of shops rather than a shop. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I had thought I should find the doors open, but they were closed, and as I stood in the wide entrance a carriage stopped outside, and a man in uniform — you know the kind of personage with ‘ Omnium ’ on his cap — flung open the door. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I contrived to enter, and walking down the shop — it was a department where they were selling ribbons and gloves and stockings and that kind of thing — came to a more spacious region devoted to picnic baskets and wicker furniture. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ I did not feel safe there, however; people were going to and fro, and I prowled restlessly about until I came upon a huge section in an upper floor containing multitudes of bedsteads, and over these I clambered, and found a resting - place at last among a huge pile of folded flock mattresses. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] The place was already lit up and agreeably warm, and I decided to remain where I was, keeping a cautious eye on the two or three sets of shopmen and customers who were meandering through the place, until closing time came. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Then I should be able, I thought, to rob the place for food and clothing, and disguised, prowl through it and examine its resources, perhaps sleep on some of the bedding. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] That seemed an acceptable plan. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] My idea was to procure clothing to make myself a muffled but acceptable figure, to get money, and then to recover my books and parcels where they awaited me, take a lodging somewhere and elaborate plans for the complete realisation of the advantages my invisibility gave me (as I still imagined) over my fellow - men. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ Closing time arrived quickly enough. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] It could not have been more than an hour after I took up my position on the mattresses before I noticed the blinds of the windows being drawn, and customers being marched doorward. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] And then a number of brisk young men began with remarkable alacrity to tidy up the goods that remained disturbed. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I left my lair as the crowds diminished, and prowled cautiously out into the less desolate parts of the shop. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I was really surprised to observe how rapidly the young men and women whipped away the goods displayed for sale during the day. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] All the boxes of goods, the hanging fabrics, the festoons of lace, the boxes of sweets in the grocery section, the displays of this and that, were being whipped down, folded up, slapped into tidy receptacles, and everything that could not be taken down and put away had sheets of some coarse stuff like sacking flung over them. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Finally all the chairs were turned up on to the counters, leaving the floor clear. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Directly each of these young people had done, he or she made promptly for the door with such an expression of animation as I have rarely observed in a shop assistant before. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Then came a lot of youngsters scattering sawdust and carrying pails and brooms. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I had to dodge to get out of the way, and as it was, my ankle got stung with the sawdust. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] For some time, wandering through the swathed and darkened departments, I could hear the brooms at work. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] And at last a good hour or more after the shop had been closed, came a noise of locking doors. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Silence came upon the place, and I found myself wandering through the vast and intricate shops, galleries, show - rooms of the place, alone. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] It was very still; in one place I remember passing near one of the Tottenham Court Road entrances and listening to the tapping of boot - heels of the passers - by. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ My first visit was to the place where I had seen stockings and gloves for sale. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] It was dark, and I had the devil of a hunt after matches, which I found at last in the drawer of the little cash desk. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Then I had to get a candle. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I had to tear down wrappings and ransack a number of boxes and drawers, but at last I managed to turn out what I sought; the box label called them lambswool pants, and lambswool vests. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Then socks, a thick comforter, and then I went to the clothing place and got trousers, a lounge jacket, an overcoat and a slouch hat — a clerical sort of hat with the brim turned down. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I began to feel a human being again, and my next thought was food. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ Upstairs was a refreshment department, and there I got cold meat. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] There was coffee still in the urn, and I lit the gas and warmed it up again, and altogether I did not do badly. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Afterwards, prowling through the place in search of blankets — I had to put up at last with a heap of down quilts — I came upon a grocery section with a lot of chocolate and candied fruits, more than was good for me indeed — and some white burgundy. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] And near that was a toy department, and I had a brilliant idea. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I found some artificial noses — dummy noses, you know, and I thought of dark spectacles. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] But Omniums had no optical department. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] My nose had been a difficulty indeed — I had thought of paint. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] But the discovery set my mind running on wigs and masks and the like. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Finally I went to sleep in a heap of down quilts, very warm and comfortable. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ My last thoughts before sleeping were the most agreeable I had had since the change. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I was in a state of physical serenity, and that was reflected in my mind. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I thought that I should be able to slip out unobserved in the morning with my clothes upon me, muffling my face with a white wrapper I had taken, purchase, with the money I had taken, spectacles and so forth, and so complete my disguise. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I lapsed into disorderly dreams of all the fantastic things that had happened during the last few days. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I saw the ugly little Jew of a landlord vociferating in his rooms; I saw his two sons marvelling, and the wrinkled old woman ’s gnarled face as she asked for her cat. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I experienced again the strange sensation of seeing the cloth disappear, and so I came round to the windy hillside and the sniffing old clergyman mumbling ‘ Earth to earth, ashes to ashes, dust to dust, ’ at my father ’s open grave. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ ‘ You also, ’ said a voice, and suddenly I was being forced towards the grave. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I struggled, shouted, appealed to the mourners, but they continued stonily following the service; the old clergyman, too, never faltered droning and sniffing through the ritual. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I realised I was invisible and inaudible, that overwhelming forces had their grip on me. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I struggled in vain, I was forced over the brink, the coffin rang hollow as I fell upon it, and the gravel came flying after me in spadefuls. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Nobody heeded me, nobody was aware of me. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I made convulsive struggles and awoke. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ The pale London dawn had come, the place was full of a chilly grey light that filtered round the edges of the window blinds. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I sat up, and for a time I could not think where this ample apartment, with its counters, its piles of rolled stuff, its heap of quilts and cushions, its iron pillars, might be. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Then, as recollection came back to me, I heard voices in conversation. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ Then far down the place, in the brighter light of some department which had already raised its blinds, I saw two men approaching. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I scrambled to my feet, looking about me for some way of escape, and even as I did so the sound of my movement made them aware of me. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I suppose they saw merely a figure moving quietly and quickly away. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] ‘ Who ’s that? ’ cried one, and ‘ Stop there! ’ [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] shouted the other. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I dashed around a corner and came full tilt — a faceless figure, mind you! —on a lanky lad of fifteen. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] He yelled and I bowled him over, rushed past him, turned another corner, and by a happy inspiration threw myself behind a counter. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] In another moment feet went running past and I heard voices shouting, ‘ All hands to the doors! ’ [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] asking what was ‘ up, ’ and giving one another advice how to catch me. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ Lying on the ground, I felt scared out of my wits. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] But — odd as it may seem — it did not occur to me at the moment to take off my clothes as I should have done. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I had made up my mind, I suppose, to get away in them, and that ruled me. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] And then down the vista of the counters came a bawling of ‘ Here he is! ’ [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ I sprang to my feet, whipped a chair off the counter, and sent it whirling at the fool who had shouted, turned, came into another round a corner, sent him spinning, and rushed up the stairs. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] He kept his footing, gave a view hallo, and came up the staircase hot after me. [/]
[Narrator] Up the staircase were piled a multitude of those bright - coloured pot things — what are they? ” “ Art pots, ” suggested Kemp. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ That ’s it! [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Art pots. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Well, I turned at the top step and swung round, plucked one out of a pile and smashed it on his silly head as he came at me. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] The whole pile of pots went headlong, and I heard shouting and footsteps running from all parts. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I made a mad rush for the refreshment place, and there was a man in white like a man cook, who took up the chase. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I made one last desperate turn and found myself among lamps and ironmongery. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I went behind the counter of this, and waited for my cook, and as he bolted in at the head of the chase, I doubled him up with a lamp. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Down he went, and I crouched down behind the counter and began whipping off my clothes as fast as I could. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Coat, jacket, trousers, shoes were all right, but a lambswool vest fits a man like a skin. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I heard more men coming, my cook was lying quiet on the other side of the counter, stunned or scared speechless, and I had to make another dash for it, like a rabbit hunted out of a wood - pile. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ ‘ This way, policeman! ’ [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I heard someone shouting. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I found myself in my bedstead storeroom again, and at the end of a wilderness of wardrobes. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I rushed among them, went flat, got rid of my vest after infinite wriggling, and stood a free man again, panting and scared, as the policeman and three of the shopmen came round the corner. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] They made a rush for the vest and pants, and collared the trousers. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] ‘ He ’s dropping his plunder, ’ said one of the young men. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] ‘ He _ must _ be somewhere here. ’ [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ But they did not find me all the same. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ I stood watching them hunt for me for a time, and cursing my ill - luck in losing the clothes. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Then I went into the refreshment - room, drank a little milk I found there, and sat down by the fire to consider my position. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ In a little while two assistants came in and began to talk over the business very excitedly and like the fools they were. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I heard a magnified account of my depredations, and other speculations as to my whereabouts. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Then I fell to scheming again. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] The insurmountable difficulty of the place, especially now it was alarmed, was to get any plunder out of it. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I went down into the warehouse to see if there was any chance of packing and addressing a parcel, but I could not understand the system of checking. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] About eleven o’clock, the snow having thawed as it fell, and the day being finer and a little warmer than the previous one, I decided that the Emporium was hopeless, and went out again, exasperated at my want of success, with only the vaguest plans of action in my mind. ” CHAPTER XXIII. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] IN DRURY LANE “ But you begin now to realise, ” said the Invisible Man, “ the full disadvantage of my condition. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I had no shelter — no covering — to get clothing was to forego all my advantage, to make myself a strange and terrible thing. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I was fasting; for to eat, to fill myself with unassimilated matter, would be to become grotesquely visible again. ” [/]
[Kemp] “ I never thought of that, ” said Kemp. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ Nor had I. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] And the snow had warned me of other dangers. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I could not go abroad in snow — it would settle on me and expose me. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Rain, too, would make me a watery outline, a glistening surface of a man — a bubble. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] And fog — I should be like a fainter bubble in a fog, a surface, a greasy glimmer of humanity. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Moreover, as I went abroad — in the London air — I gathered dirt about my ankles, floating smuts and dust upon my skin. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I did not know how long it would be before I should become visible from that cause also. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] But I saw clearly it could not be for long. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ Not in London at any rate. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ I went into the slums towards Great Portland Street, and found myself at the end of the street in which I had lodged. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I did not go that way, because of the crowd halfway down it opposite to the still smoking ruins of the house I had fired. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] My most immediate problem was to get clothing. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] What to do with my face puzzled me. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Then I saw in one of those little miscellaneous shops — news, sweets, toys, stationery, belated Christmas tomfoolery, and so forth — an array of masks and noses. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I realised that problem was solved. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] In a flash I saw my course. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I turned about, no longer aimless, and went — circuitously in order to avoid the busy ways, towards the back streets north of the Strand; for I remembered, though not very distinctly where, that some theatrical costumiers had shops in that district. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ The day was cold, with a nipping wind down the northward running streets. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I walked fast to avoid being overtaken. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Every crossing was a danger, every passenger a thing to watch alertly. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] One man as I was about to pass him at the top of Bedford Street, turned upon me abruptly and came into me, sending me into the road and almost under the wheel of a passing hansom. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] The verdict of the cab - rank was that he had had some sort of stroke. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I was so unnerved by this encounter that I went into Covent Garden Market and sat down for some time in a quiet corner by a stall of violets, panting and trembling. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I found I had caught a fresh cold, and had to turn out after a time lest my sneezes should attract attention. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ At last I reached the object of my quest, a dirty, fly - blown little shop in a by - way near Drury Lane, with a window full of tinsel robes, sham jewels, wigs, slippers, dominoes and theatrical photographs. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] The shop was old - fashioned and low and dark, and the house rose above it for four storeys, dark and dismal. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I peered through the window and, seeing no one within, entered. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] The opening of the door set a clanking bell ringing. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I left it open, and walked round a bare costume stand, into a corner behind a cheval glass. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] For a minute or so no one came. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Then I heard heavy feet striding across a room, and a man appeared down the shop. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ My plans were now perfectly definite. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I proposed to make my way into the house, secrete myself upstairs, watch my opportunity, and when everything was quiet, rummage out a wig, mask, spectacles, and costume, and go into the world, perhaps a grotesque but still a credible figure. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] And incidentally of course I could rob the house of any available money. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ The man who had just entered the shop was a short, slight, hunched, beetle - browed man, with long arms and very short bandy legs. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Apparently I had interrupted a meal. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] He stared about the shop with an expression of expectation. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] This gave way to surprise, and then to anger, as he saw the shop empty. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] ‘ Damn the boys! ’ [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] he said. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] He went to stare up and down the street. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] He came in again in a minute, kicked the door to with his foot spitefully, and went muttering back to the house door. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ I came forward to follow him, and at the noise of my movement he stopped dead. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I did so too, startled by his quickness of ear. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] He slammed the house door in my face. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ I stood hesitating. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Suddenly I heard his quick footsteps returning, and the door reopened. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] He stood looking about the shop like one who was still not satisfied. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Then, murmuring to himself, he examined the back of the counter and peered behind some fixtures. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Then he stood doubtful. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] He had left the house door open [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] and I slipped into the inner room. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ It was a queer little room, poorly furnished and with a number of big masks in the corner. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] On the table was his belated breakfast, and it was a confoundedly exasperating thing for me, Kemp, to have to sniff his coffee and stand watching while he came in and resumed his meal. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] And his table manners were irritating. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Three doors opened into the little room, one going upstairs and one down, but they were all shut. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I could not get out of the room while he was there; I could scarcely move because of his alertness, and there was a draught down my back. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Twice I strangled a sneeze just in time. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ The spectacular quality of my sensations was curious and novel, but for all that I was heartily tired and angry long before he had done his eating. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] But at last he made an end and putting his beggarly crockery on the black tin tray upon which he had had his teapot, and gathering all the crumbs up on the mustard stained cloth, he took the whole lot of things after him. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] His burden prevented his shutting the door behind him — as he would have done; I never saw such a man for shutting doors — and I followed him into a very dirty underground kitchen and scullery. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I had the pleasure of seeing him begin to wash up, and then, finding no good in keeping down there, and the brick floor being cold on my feet, I returned upstairs and sat in his chair by the fire. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] It was burning low, and scarcely thinking, I put on a little coal. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] The noise of this brought him up at once, and he stood aglare. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] He peered about the room and was within an ace of touching me. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Even after that examination, he scarcely seemed satisfied. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] He stopped in the doorway and took a final inspection before he went down. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ I waited in the little parlour for an age, and at last he came up and opened the upstairs door. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I just managed to get by him. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ On the staircase he stopped suddenly, so that I very nearly blundered into him. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] He stood looking back right into my face and listening. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] ‘ I could have sworn, ’ he said. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] His long hairy hand pulled at his lower lip. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] His eye went up and down the staircase. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Then he grunted and went on up again. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ His hand was on the handle of a door, and then he stopped again with the same puzzled anger on his face. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] He was becoming aware of the faint sounds of my movements about him. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] The man must have had diabolically acute hearing. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] He suddenly flashed into rage. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] ‘ If there ’s anyone in this house — ’ he cried with an oath, and left the threat unfinished. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] He put his hand in his pocket, failed to find what he wanted, and rushing past me went blundering noisily and pugnaciously downstairs. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] But I did not follow him. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I sat on the head of the staircase until his return. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ Presently he came up again, still muttering. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] He opened the door of the room, and before I could enter, slammed it in my face. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ I resolved to explore the house, and spent some time in doing so as noiselessly as possible. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] The house was very old and tumble - down, damp so that the paper in the attics was peeling from the walls, and rat infested. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Some of the door handles were stiff and I was afraid to turn them. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Several rooms I did inspect were unfurnished, and others were littered with theatrical lumber, bought second - hand, I judged, from its appearance. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] In one room next to his I found a lot of old clothes. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I began routing among these, and in my eagerness forgot again the evident sharpness of his ears. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I heard a stealthy footstep and, looking up just in time, saw him peering in at the tumbled heap and holding an old - fashioned revolver in his hand. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I stood perfectly still while he stared about open - mouthed and suspicious. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] ‘ It must have been her, ’ he said slowly. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] ‘ Damn her! ’ “ He shut the door quietly, and immediately I heard the key turn in the lock. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Then his footsteps retreated. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I realised abruptly that I was locked in. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] For a minute I did not know what to do. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I walked from door to window and back, and stood perplexed. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] A gust of anger came upon me. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] But I decided to inspect the clothes before I did anything further, and my first attempt brought down a pile from an upper shelf. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] This brought him back, more sinister than ever. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] That time he actually touched me, jumped back with amazement and stood astonished in the middle of the room. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ Presently he calmed a little. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] ‘ Rats, ’ he said in an undertone, fingers on lips. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] He was evidently a little scared. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I edged quietly out of the room, but a plank creaked. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Then the infernal little brute started going all over the house, revolver in hand and locking door after door and pocketing the keys. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] When I realised what he was up to I had a fit of rage — I could hardly control myself sufficiently to watch my opportunity. [/]
[Narrator] By this time I knew he was alone in the house, and so I made no more ado, but knocked him on the head. ” “ Knocked him on the head? ” exclaimed Kemp. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ Yes — stunned him — as he was going downstairs. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Hit him from behind with a stool that stood on the landing. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] He went downstairs like a bag of old boots. ” [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ But — I say! [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] The common conventions of humanity — ” “ Are all very well for common people. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] But the point was, Kemp, that I had to get out of that house in a disguise without his seeing me. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I could n’t think of any other way of doing it. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] And then I gagged him with a Louis Quatorze vest and tied him up in a sheet. ” “ Tied him up in a sheet! ” “ Made a sort of bag of it. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] It was rather a good idea to keep the idiot scared and quiet, and a devilish hard thing to get out of — head away from the string. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] My dear Kemp, it ’s no good your sitting glaring as though I was a murderer. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] It had to be done. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] He had his revolver. [/]
[Narrator] If once he saw me he would be able to describe me — ” “ But still, ” said Kemp, “ in England — to - day. [/]
[Narrator] And the man was in his own house, and you were — well, robbing. ” “ Robbing! [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Confound it! [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] You ’ll call me a thief next! [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Surely, Kemp, you ’re not fool enough to dance on the old strings. [/]
[Narrator] Ca n’t you see my position? ” “ And his too, ” said Kemp. [/]
[Narrator] The Invisible Man stood up sharply. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ What do you mean to say? ” Kemp ’s face grew a trifle hard. [/]
[Narrator] He was about to speak and checked himself. [/]
[Kemp] “ I suppose, after all, ” he said with a sudden change of manner, “ the thing had to be done. [/]
[Kemp] You were in a fix. [/]
[Narrator] But still — ” “ Of course I was in a fix — an infernal fix. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] And he made me wild too — hunting me about the house, fooling about with his revolver, locking and unlocking doors. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] He was simply exasperating. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] You do n’t blame me, do you? [/]
[Narrator] You do n’t blame me? ” “ I never blame anyone, ” said Kemp. [/]
[Kemp] “ It ’s quite out of fashion. [/]
[Narrator] What did you do next? ” “ I was hungry. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Downstairs I found a loaf and some rank cheese — more than sufficient to satisfy my hunger. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I took some brandy and water, and then went up past my impromptu bag — he was lying quite still — to the room containing the old clothes. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] This looked out upon the street, two lace curtains brown with dirt guarding the window. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I went and peered out through their interstices. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Outside the day was bright — by contrast with the brown shadows of the dismal house in which I found myself, dazzlingly bright. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] A brisk traffic was going by, fruit carts, a hansom, a four - wheeler with a pile of boxes, a fishmonger ’s cart. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I turned with spots of colour swimming before my eyes to the shadowy fixtures behind me. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] My excitement was giving place to a clear apprehension of my position again. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] The room was full of a faint scent of benzoline, used, I suppose, in cleaning the garments. [/]
[Kemp] “ I began a systematic search of the place. [/]
[Kemp] I should judge the hunchback had been alone in the house for some time. [/]
[Kemp] He was a curious person. [/]
[Kemp] Everything that could possibly be of service to me I collected in the clothes storeroom, and then I made a deliberate selection. [/]
[Kemp] I found a handbag I thought a suitable possession, and some powder, rouge, and sticking - plaster. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ I had thought of painting and powdering my face and all that there was to show of me, in order to render myself visible, but the disadvantage of this lay in the fact that I should require turpentine and other appliances and a considerable amount of time before I could vanish again. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Finally I chose a mask of the better type, slightly grotesque but not more so than many human beings, dark glasses, greyish whiskers, and a wig. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I could find no underclothing, but that I could buy subsequently, and for the time I swathed myself in calico dominoes and some white cashmere scarfs. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I could find no socks, but the hunchback ’s boots were rather a loose fit and sufficed. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] In a desk in the shop were three sovereigns and about thirty shillings ’ worth of silver, and in a locked cupboard I burst in the inner room were eight pounds in gold. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I could go forth into the world again, equipped. [/]
[Kemp] “ Then came a curious hesitation. [/]
[Kemp] Was my appearance really credible? [/]
[Kemp] I tried myself with a little bedroom looking - glass, inspecting myself from every point of view to discover any forgotten chink, but it all seemed sound. [/]
[Kemp] I was grotesque to the theatrical pitch, a stage miser, but I was certainly not a physical impossibility. [/]
[Kemp] Gathering confidence, I took my looking - glass down into the shop, pulled down the shop blinds, and surveyed myself from every point of view with the help of the cheval glass in the corner. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ I spent some minutes screwing up my courage and then unlocked the shop door and marched out into the street, leaving the little man to get out of his sheet again when he liked. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] In five minutes a dozen turnings intervened between me and the costumier ’s shop. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] No one appeared to notice me very pointedly. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] My last difficulty seemed overcome. ” [/]
[Narrator] He stopped again. [/]
[Kemp] “ And you troubled no more about the hunchback? ” said Kemp. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ No, ” said the Invisible Man. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ Nor have I heard what became of him. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I suppose he untied himself or kicked himself out. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] The knots were pretty tight. ” [/]
[Narrator] He became silent and went to the window and stared out. [/]
[Narrator] “ What happened when you went out into the Strand? ” “ Oh! —disillusionment again. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I thought my troubles were over. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Practically I thought I had impunity to do whatever I chose, everything — save to give away my secret. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] So I thought. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Whatever I did, whatever the consequences might be, was nothing to me. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I had merely to fling aside my garments and vanish. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] No person could hold me. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I could take my money where I found it. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I decided to treat myself to a sumptuous feast, and then put up at a good hotel, and accumulate a new outfit of property. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I felt amazingly confident; it ’s not particularly pleasant recalling that I was an ass. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I went into a place and was already ordering lunch, when it occurred to me that I could not eat unless I exposed my invisible face. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I finished ordering the lunch, told the man I should be back in ten minutes, and went out exasperated. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I do n’t know if you have ever been disappointed in your appetite. ” [/]
[Kemp] “ Not quite so badly, ” said Kemp, “ but I can imagine it. ” [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ I could have smashed the silly devils. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] At last, faint with the desire for tasteful food, I went into another place and demanded a private room. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] ‘ I am disfigured, ’ I said. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] ‘ Badly. ’ [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] They looked at me curiously, but of course it was not their affair — and so at last I got my lunch. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] It was not particularly well served, but it sufficed; and when I had had it, I sat over a cigar, trying to plan my line of action. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] And outside a snowstorm was beginning. [/]
[Kemp] “ The more I thought it over, Kemp, the more I realised what a helpless absurdity an Invisible Man was — in a cold and dirty climate and a crowded civilised city. [/]
[Kemp] Before I made this mad experiment I had dreamt of a thousand advantages. [/]
[Kemp] That afternoon it seemed all disappointment. [/]
[Kemp] I went over the heads of the things a man reckons desirable. [/]
[Kemp] No doubt invisibility made it possible to get them, but it made it impossible to enjoy them when they are got. [/]
[Kemp] Ambition — what is the good of pride of place when you can not appear there? [/]
[Kemp] What is the good of the love of woman when her name must needs be Delilah? [/]
[Kemp] I have no taste for politics, for the blackguardisms of fame, for philanthropy, for sport. [/]
[Kemp] What was I to do? [/]
[Kemp] And for this I had become a wrapped - up mystery, a swathed and bandaged caricature of a man! ” [/]
[Narrator] He paused, and his attitude suggested a roving glance at the window. [/]
[Kemp] “ But how did you get to Iping? ” said Kemp, anxious to keep his guest busy talking. [/]
[Kemp] “ I went there to work. [/]
[Kemp] I had one hope. [/]
[Kemp] It was a half idea! [/]
[Kemp] I have it still. [/]
[Kemp] It is a full blown idea now. [/]
[Kemp] A way of getting back! [/]
[Kemp] Of restoring what I have done. [/]
[Kemp] When I choose. [/]
[Kemp] When I have done all I mean to do invisibly. [/]
[Kemp] And that is what I chiefly want to talk to you about now. ” [/]
[Kemp] “ You went straight to Iping? ” “ Yes. [/]
[Kemp] I had simply to get my three volumes of memoranda and my cheque - book, my luggage and underclothing, order a quantity of chemicals to work out this idea of mine — I will show you the calculations as soon as I get my books — and then I started. [/]
[Kemp] Jove! [/]
[Kemp] I remember the snowstorm now, and the accursed bother it was to keep the snow from damping my pasteboard nose. ” [/]
[Kemp] “ At the end, ” said Kemp, “ the day before yesterday, when they found you out, you rather — to judge by the papers — ” “ I did. [/]
[Kemp] Rather. [/]
[Kemp] Did I kill that fool of a constable? ” “ No, ” said Kemp. [/]
[Kemp] “ He ’s expected to recover. ” [/]
[Kemp] “ That ’s his luck, then. [/]
[Kemp] I clean lost my temper, the fools! [/]
[Kemp] Why could n’t they leave me alone? [/]
[Kemp] And that grocer lout? ” “ There are no deaths expected, ” said Kemp. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ I do n’t know about that tramp of mine, ” said the Invisible Man, with an unpleasant laugh. [/]
[Kemp] “ By Heaven, Kemp, you do n’t know what rage _ is _! .. . [/]
[Kemp] To have worked for years, to have planned and plotted, and then to get some fumbling purblind idiot messing across your course! .. . [/]
[Kemp] Every conceivable sort of silly creature that has ever been created has been sent to cross me. [/]
[Kemp] “ If I have much more of it, I shall go wild — I shall start mowing ’em. [/]
[Kemp] “ As it is, they ’ve made things a thousand times more difficult. ” [/]
[Kemp] “ No doubt it ’s exasperating, ” said Kemp, drily. CHAPTER XXIV. [/]
[Kemp] THE PLAN THAT FAILED “ But now, ” said Kemp, with a side glance out of the window, “ what are we to do? ” [/]
[Narrator] He moved nearer his guest as he spoke in such a manner as to prevent the possibility of a sudden glimpse of the three men who were advancing up the hill road — with an intolerable slowness, as it seemed to Kemp. [/]
[Kemp] “ What were you planning to do when you were heading for Port Burdock? _ [/]
[Kemp] Had _ you any plan? ” “ I was going to clear out of the country. [/]
[Kemp] But I have altered that plan rather since seeing you. [/]
[Kemp] I thought it would be wise, now the weather is hot and invisibility possible, to make for the South. [/]
[Kemp] Especially as my secret was known, and everyone would be on the lookout for a masked and muffled man. [/]
[Kemp] You have a line of steamers from here to France. [/]
[Kemp] My idea was to get aboard one and run the risks of the passage. [/]
[Kemp] Thence I could go by train into Spain, or else get to Algiers. [/]
[Kemp] It would not be difficult. [/]
[Kemp] There a man might always be invisible — and yet live. [/]
[Kemp] And do things. [/]

[Kemp] “ That ’s clear. ” [/]

[Kemp] He _ has _ hidden my books, Kemp. [/]
[Kemp] Hidden my books! [/]
[Kemp] If I can lay my hands on him! ” “ Best plan to get the books out of him first. ” [/]
[Kemp] “ But where is he? [/]
[Narrator] Do you know? ” “ He ’s in the town police station, locked up, by his own request, in the strongest cell in the place. ” “ Cur! ” said the Invisible Man. [/]

[Kemp] “ Certainly, ” said Kemp, a little nervously, wondering if he heard footsteps outside. [/]
[Kemp] “ Certainly we must get those books. [/]
[Kemp] But that wo n’t be difficult, if he does n’t know they ’re for you. ” [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ No, ” said the Invisible Man, and thought. [/]
[Narrator] Kemp tried to think of something to keep the talk going, but the Invisible Man resumed of his own accord. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ Blundering into your house, Kemp, ” he said, “ changes all my plans. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] For you are a man that can understand. [/]

[Narrator] he asked abruptly. [/]
[Narrator] Kemp hesitated. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ That was implied, ” he said. [/]
[Griffin] “ No one? ” insisted Griffin. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ Not a soul. ” [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ Ah! [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Now — ” The Invisible Man stood up, and sticking his arms akimbo began to pace the study. [/]

[TheInvisibleMan] I have wasted strength, time, opportunities. [/]

[TheInvisibleMan] With a confederate, with food and rest — a thousand things are possible. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ Hitherto I have gone on vague lines. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] We have to consider all that invisibility means, all that it does not mean. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] It means little advantage for eavesdropping and so forth — one makes sounds. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] It ’s of little help — a little help perhaps — in housebreaking and so forth. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Once you ’ve caught me you could easily imprison me. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] But on the other hand I am hard to catch. [/]

[TheInvisibleMan] It ’s particularly useful, therefore, in killing. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] I can walk round a man, whatever weapon he has, choose my point, strike as I like. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Dodge as I like. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] Escape as I like. ” Kemp ’s hand went to his moustache. [/]
[Narrator] Was that a movement downstairs? [/]

[Kemp] “ I ’m listening to your plan, Griffin, but I ’m not agreeing, mind. _ [/]
[Narrator] Why _ killing? ” “ Not wanton killing, but a judicious slaying. [/]

[TheInvisibleMan] Yes; no doubt it ’s startling. [/]

[TheInvisibleMan] He can do that in a thousand ways — scraps of paper thrust under doors would suffice. [/]
[Narrator] And all who disobey his orders he must kill, and kill all who would defend them. ” “ Humph! ” said Kemp, no longer listening to Griffin but to the sound of his front door opening and closing. [/]

[Kemp] And then suddenly, “ Hush! [/]
[Kemp] What ’s that downstairs? ” “ Nothing, ” said Kemp, and suddenly began to speak loud and fast. [/]
[Kemp] “ I do n’t agree to this, Griffin, ” he said. [/]

[Kemp] Why dream of playing a game against the race? [/]
[Kemp] How can you hope to gain happiness? [/]
[Kemp] Do n’t be a lone wolf. [/]

[TheInvisibleMan] “ There are footsteps coming upstairs, ” he said in a low voice. [/]
[Kemp] “ Nonsense, ” said Kemp. [/]
[TheInvisibleMan] “ Let me see, ” said the Invisible Man, and advanced, arm extended, to the door. [/]
[Narrator] And then things happened very swiftly. [/]
[Narrator] Kemp hesitated for a second and then moved to intercept him. [/]
[Narrator] The Invisible Man started and stood still. [/]
[TheVoice] “ Traitor! ” cried the Voice, and suddenly the dressing - gown opened, and sitting down the Unseen began to disrobe. [/]

[Narrator] As it opened, there came a sound of hurrying feet downstairs and voices. [/]

[Narrator] The key was outside and ready. [/]
[Narrator] In another moment Griffin would have been alone in the belvedere study, a prisoner. [/]
[Narrator] Save for one little thing. [/]
[Narrator] The key had been slipped in hastily that morning. [/]
[Narrator] As Kemp slammed the door it fell noisily upon the carpet. [/]

[Narrator] He tried to grip the door handle with both hands. [/]
[Narrator] For a moment he stood lugging. [/]
[Narrator] Then the door gave six inches. [/]
[Narrator] But he got it closed again. [/]
[Narrator] The second time it was jerked a foot wide, and the dressing - gown came wedging itself into the opening. [/]

[Narrator] He was staring aghast at the sudden appearance of Kemp, followed by the extraordinary sight of clothing tossing empty in the air. [/]
[Narrator] He saw Kemp felled, and struggling to his feet. [/]
[Narrator] He saw him rush forward, and go down again, felled like an ox. [/]
[Narrator] Then suddenly he was struck violently. [/]
[Narrator] By nothing! [/]
[Narrator] A vast weight, it seemed, leapt upon him, and he was hurled headlong down the staircase, with a grip on his throat and a knee in his groin. [/]
[Narrator] An invisible foot trod on his back, a ghostly patter passed downstairs, he heard the two police officers in the hall shout and run, and the front door of the house slammed violently. [/]
[Narrator] He rolled over and sat up staring. [/]
[Narrator] He saw, staggering down the staircase, Kemp, dusty and disheveled, one side of his face white from a blow, his lip bleeding, and a pink dressing - gown and some underclothing held in his arms. [/]
[Kemp] “ My God! ” cried Kemp, “ the game ’s up! [/]
[Kemp] He ’s gone! ” CHAPTER XXV. [/]
[Narrator] THE HUNTING OF THE INVISIBLE MAN For a space Kemp was too inarticulate to make Adye understand the swift things that had just happened. [/]
[Narrator] They stood on the landing, Kemp speaking swiftly, the grotesque swathings of Griffin still on his arm. [/]

[Kemp] “ He is mad, ” said Kemp; “ inhuman. [/]
[Kemp] He is pure selfishness. [/]

[Kemp] He will kill them unless we can prevent him. [/]

[Kemp] Nothing can stop him. [/]

[Adye] it ’s urgent. [/]

[Kemp] “ Get dogs. [/]
[Kemp] They do n’t see him, but they wind him. [/]
[Kemp] Get dogs. ” [/]

[Narrator] If he goes too far. .. . ” “ The man ’s become inhuman, I tell you, ” said Kemp. [/]
