[Kemp] Our only chance is to be ahead. [/]
[Kemp] He has cut himself off from his kind. [/]
[Kemp] His blood be upon his own head. ” CHAPTER XXVI. [/]
[Narrator] THE WICKSTEED MURDER The Invisible Man seems to have rushed out of Kemp ’s house in a state of blind fury. [/]
[Narrator] A little child playing near Kemp ’s gateway was violently caught up and thrown aside, so that its ankle was broken, and thereafter for some hours the Invisible Man passed out of human perceptions. [/]
[Narrator] No one knows where he went nor what he did. [/]
[Narrator] But one can imagine him hurrying through the hot June forenoon, up the hill and on to the open downland behind Port Burdock, raging and despairing at his intolerable fate, and sheltering at last, heated and weary, amid the thickets of Hintondean, to piece together again his shattered schemes against his species. [/]
[Narrator] That seems the most probable refuge for him, for there it was he re - asserted himself in a grimly tragical manner about two in the afternoon. [/]
[Narrator] One wonders what his state of mind may have been during that time, and what plans he devised. [/]
[Narrator] No doubt he was almost ecstatically exasperated by Kemp ’s treachery, and though we may be able to understand the motives that led to that deceit, we may still imagine and even sympathise a little with the fury the attempted surprise must have occasioned. [/]
[Narrator] Perhaps something of the stunned astonishment of his Oxford Street experiences may have returned to him, for he had evidently counted on Kemp ’s co - operation in his brutal dream of a terrorised world. [/]
[Narrator] At any rate he vanished from human ken about midday, and no living witness can tell what he did until about half - past two. [/]
[Narrator] It was a fortunate thing, perhaps, for humanity, but for him it was a fatal inaction. [/]
[Narrator] During that time a growing multitude of men scattered over the countryside were busy. [/]
[Narrator] In the morning he had still been simply a legend, a terror; in the afternoon, by virtue chiefly of Kemp ’s drily worded proclamation, he was presented as a tangible antagonist, to be wounded, captured, or overcome, and the countryside began organising itself with inconceivable rapidity. [/]
[Narrator] By two o’clock even he might still have removed himself out of the district by getting aboard a train, but after two that became impossible. [/]
[Narrator] Every passenger train along the lines on a great parallelogram between Southampton, Manchester, Brighton and Horsham, travelled with locked doors, and the goods traffic was almost entirely suspended. [/]
[Narrator] And in a great circle of twenty miles round Port Burdock, men armed with guns and bludgeons were presently setting out in groups of three and four, with dogs, to beat the roads and fields. [/]
[Narrator] Mounted policemen rode along the country lanes, stopping at every cottage and warning the people to lock up their houses, and keep indoors unless they were armed, and all the elementary schools had broken up by three o’clock, and the children, scared and keeping together in groups, were hurrying home. [/]
[Narrator] Kemp ’s proclamation — signed indeed by Adye — was posted over almost the whole district by four or five o’clock in the afternoon. [/]
[Narrator] It gave briefly but clearly all the conditions of the struggle, the necessity of keeping the Invisible Man from food and sleep, the necessity for incessant watchfulness and for a prompt attention to any evidence of his movements. [/]
[Narrator] And so swift and decided was the action of the authorities, so prompt and universal was the belief in this strange being, that before nightfall an area of several hundred square miles was in a stringent state of siege. [/]
[Narrator] And before nightfall, too, a thrill of horror went through the whole watching nervous countryside. [/]
[Narrator] Going from whispering mouth to mouth, swift and certain over the length and breadth of the country, passed the story of the murder of Mr. Wicksteed. [/]
[Narrator] If our supposition that the Invisible Man ’s refuge was the Hintondean thickets, then we must suppose that in the early afternoon he sallied out again bent upon some project that involved the use of a weapon. [/]
[Narrator] We can not know what the project was, but the evidence that he had the iron rod in hand before he met Wicksteed is to me at least overwhelming. [/]
[Narrator] Of course we can know nothing of the details of that encounter. [/]
[Narrator] It occurred on the edge of a gravel pit, not two hundred yards from Lord Burdock ’s lodge gate. [/]
[Narrator] Everything points to a desperate struggle — the trampled ground, the numerous wounds Mr. Wicksteed received, his splintered walking - stick; but why the attack was made, save in a murderous frenzy, it is impossible to imagine. [/]
[Narrator] Indeed the theory of madness is almost unavoidable. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Wicksteed was a man of forty - five or forty - six, steward to Lord Burdock, of inoffensive habits and appearance, the very last person in the world to provoke such a terrible antagonist. [/]
[Narrator] Against him it would seem the Invisible Man used an iron rod dragged from a broken piece of fence. [/]
[Narrator] He stopped this quiet man, going quietly home to his midday meal, attacked him, beat down his feeble defences, broke his arm, felled him, and smashed his head to a jelly. [/]
[Narrator] Of course, he must have dragged this rod out of the fencing before he met his victim — he must have been carrying it ready in his hand. [/]
[Narrator] Only two details beyond what has already been stated seem to bear on the matter. [/]
[Narrator] One is the circumstance that the gravel pit was not in Mr. Wicksteed ’s direct path home, but nearly a couple of hundred yards out of his way. [/]
[ALittleGirl] The other is the assertion of a little girl to the effect that, going to her afternoon school, she saw the murdered man “ trotting ” in a peculiar manner across a field towards the gravel pit. [/]
[Narrator] Her pantomime of his action suggests a man pursuing something on the ground before him and striking at it ever and again with his walking - stick. [/]
[Narrator] She was the last person to see him alive. [/]
[Narrator] He passed out of her sight to his death, the struggle being hidden from her only by a clump of beech trees and a slight depression in the ground. [/]
[Narrator] Now this, to the present writer ’s mind at least, lifts the murder out of the realm of the absolutely wanton. [/]
[Narrator] We may imagine that Griffin had taken the rod as a weapon indeed, but without any deliberate intention of using it in murder. [/]
[Narrator] Wicksteed may then have come by and noticed this rod inexplicably moving through the air. [/]
[Narrator] Without any thought of the Invisible Man — for Port Burdock is ten miles away — he may have pursued it. [/]
[Narrator] It is quite conceivable that he may not even have heard of the Invisible Man. [/]
[Narrator] One can then imagine the Invisible Man making off — quietly in order to avoid discovering his presence in the neighbourhood, and Wicksteed, excited and curious, pursuing this unaccountably locomotive object — finally striking at it. [/]
[Narrator] No doubt the Invisible Man could easily have distanced his middle - aged pursuer under ordinary circumstances, but the position in which Wicksteed ’s body was found suggests that he had the ill luck to drive his quarry into a corner between a drift of stinging nettles and the gravel pit. [/]
[Narrator] To those who appreciate the extraordinary irascibility of the Invisible Man, the rest of the encounter will be easy to imagine. [/]
[Narrator] But this is pure hypothesis. [/]
[Narrator] The only undeniable facts — for stories of children are often unreliable — are the discovery of Wicksteed ’s body, done to death, and of the blood - stained iron rod flung among the nettles. [/]
[Narrator] The abandonment of the rod by Griffin, suggests that in the emotional excitement of the affair, the purpose for which he took it — if he had a purpose — was abandoned. [/]
[Narrator] He was certainly an intensely egotistical and unfeeling man, but the sight of his victim, his first victim, bloody and pitiful at his feet, may have released some long pent fountain of remorse which for a time may have flooded whatever scheme of action he had contrived. [/]
[Narrator] After the murder of Mr. Wicksteed, he would seem to have struck across the country towards the downland. [/]
[Narrator] There is a story of a voice heard about sunset by a couple of men in a field near Fern Bottom. [/]
[Narrator] It was wailing and laughing, sobbing and groaning, and ever and again it shouted. [/]
[Narrator] It must have been queer hearing. [/]
[Narrator] It drove up across the middle of a clover field and died away towards the hills. [/]
[Narrator] That afternoon the Invisible Man must have learnt something of the rapid use Kemp had made of his confidences. [/]
[Narrator] He must have found houses locked and secured; he may have loitered about railway stations and prowled about inns, and no doubt he read the proclamations and realised something of the nature of the campaign against him. [/]
[Narrator] And as the evening advanced, the fields became dotted here and there with groups of three or four men, and noisy with the yelping of dogs. [/]
[Narrator] These men - hunters had particular instructions in the case of an encounter as to the way they should support one another. [/]
[Narrator] But he avoided them all. [/]
[Narrator] We may understand something of his exasperation, and it could have been none the less because he himself had supplied the information that was being used so remorselessly against him. [/]
[Narrator] For that day at least he lost heart; for nearly twenty - four hours, save when he turned on Wicksteed, he was a hunted man. [/]
[Narrator] In the night, he must have eaten and slept; for in the morning he was himself again, active, powerful, angry, and malignant, prepared for his last great struggle against the world. [/]
[Narrator] CHAPTER XXVII. [/]
[Narrator] THE SIEGE OF KEMP ’S HOUSE Kemp read a strange missive, written in pencil on a greasy sheet of paper. [/]
[Kemp] “ You have been amazingly energetic and clever, ” this letter ran, “ though what you stand to gain by it I can not imagine. [/]
[Kemp] You are against me. [/]
[Kemp] For a whole day you have chased me; you have tried to rob me of a night ’s rest. [/]
[Kemp] But I have had food in spite of you, I have slept in spite of you, and the game is only beginning. [/]
[Kemp] The game is only beginning. [/]
[Kemp] There is nothing for it, but to start the Terror. [/]
[Kemp] This announces the first day of the Terror. [/]
[Kemp] Port Burdock is no longer under the Queen, tell your Colonel of Police, and the rest of them; it is under me — the Terror! [/]
[Kemp] This is day one of year one of the new epoch — the Epoch of the Invisible Man. [/]
[Kemp] I am Invisible Man the First. [/]
[Kemp] To begin with the rule will be easy. [/]
[Kemp] The first day there will be one execution for the sake of example — a man named Kemp. [/]
[Kemp] Death starts for him to - day. [/]
[Kemp] He may lock himself away, hide himself away, get guards about him, put on armour if he likes — Death, the unseen Death, is coming. [/]
[Kemp] Let him take precautions; it will impress my people. [/]
[Kemp] Death starts from the pillar box by midday. [/]
[Kemp] The letter will fall in as the postman comes along, then off! [/]
[Kemp] The game begins. [/]
[Kemp] Death starts. [/]
[Kemp] Help him not, my people, lest Death fall upon you also. [/]
[Kemp] To - day Kemp is to die. ” [/]

[Kemp] “ That ’s his voice! [/]
[Kemp] And he means it. ” [/]

[Kemp] to pay. ” [/]
[Narrator] He got up slowly, leaving his lunch unfinished — the letter had come by the one o’clock post — and went into his study. [/]
[Narrator] He rang for his housekeeper, and told her to go round the house at once, examine all the fastenings of the windows, and close all the shutters. [/]
[Narrator] He closed the shutters of his study himself. [/]
[Narrator] From a locked drawer in his bedroom he took a little revolver, examined it carefully, and put it into the pocket of his lounge jacket. [/]
[Narrator] He wrote a number of brief notes, one to Colonel Adye, gave them to his servant to take, with explicit instructions as to her way of leaving the house. [/]
[Kemp] “ There is no danger, ” he said, and added a mental reservation, “ to you. ” [/]
[Narrator] He remained meditative for a space after doing this, and then returned to his cooling lunch. [/]
[Narrator] He ate with gaps of thought. [/]
[Narrator] Finally he struck the table sharply. [/]
[Kemp] “ We will have him! ” [/]
[Kemp] he said; “ and I am the bait. [/]
[Kemp] He will come too far. ” [/]
[Narrator] He went up to the belvedere, carefully shutting every door after him. [/]
[Kemp] “ It ’s a game, ” he said, “ an odd game — but the chances are all for me, Mr. Griffin, in spite of your invisibility. [/]
[Kemp] Griffin _ contra mundum _. .. with a vengeance. ” [/]
[Narrator] He stood at the window staring at the hot hillside. [/]
[Kemp] “ He must get food every day — and I do n’t envy him. [/]
[Kemp] Did he really sleep last night? [/]
[Kemp] Out in the open somewhere — secure from collisions. [/]
[Kemp] I wish we could get some good cold wet weather instead of the heat. [/]
[Kemp] “ He may be watching me now. ” [/]
[Narrator] He went close to the window. [/]
[Narrator] Something rapped smartly against the brickwork over the frame, and made him start violently back. [/]

[Narrator] But it was five minutes before he went to the window again. [/]

[Narrator] A familiar voice hailed him. [/]

[Narrator] The Voice was on his left front, he thought. [/]

[Narrator] CHAPTER XXVIII. [/]
[Narrator] THE HUNTER HUNTED Mr. Heelas, Mr. Kemp ’s nearest neighbour among the villa holders, was asleep in his summer house when the siege of Kemp ’s house began. [/]
[MrHeelas] Mr. Heelas was one of the sturdy minority who refused to believe “ in all this nonsense ” about an Invisible Man. [/]
[Narrator] His wife, however, as he was subsequently to be reminded, did. [/]
[Narrator] He insisted upon walking about his garden just as if nothing was the matter, and he went to sleep in the afternoon in accordance with the custom of years. [/]
[Narrator] He slept through the smashing of the windows, and then woke up suddenly with a curious persuasion of something wrong. [/]
[Narrator] He looked across at Kemp ’s house, rubbed his eyes and looked again. [/]
[Narrator] Then he put his feet to the ground, and sat listening. [/]
[Narrator] He said he was damned, but still the strange thing was visible. [/]
[Narrator] The house looked as though it had been deserted for weeks — after a violent riot. [/]
[Narrator] Every window was broken, and every window, save those of the belvedere study, was blinded by the internal shutters. [/]
[MrHeelas] “ I could have sworn it was all right”—he looked at his watch—“twenty minutes ago. ” [/]
[Narrator] He became aware of a measured concussion and the clash of glass, far away in the distance. [/]
[Narrator] And then, as he sat open - mouthed, came a still more wonderful thing. [/]
[Narrator] The shutters of the drawing - room window were flung open violently, and the housemaid in her outdoor hat and garments, appeared struggling in a frantic manner to throw up the sash. [/]
[Narrator] Suddenly a man appeared beside her, helping her — Dr. Kemp! [/]
[Narrator] In another moment the window was open, and the housemaid was struggling out; she pitched forward and vanished among the shrubs. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Heelas stood up, exclaiming vaguely and vehemently at all these wonderful things. [/]
[Narrator] He saw Kemp stand on the sill, spring from the window, and reappear almost instantaneously running along a path in the shrubbery and stooping as he ran, like a man who evades observation. [/]
[Narrator] He vanished behind a laburnum, and appeared again clambering over a fence that abutted on the open down. [/]
[Narrator] In a second he had tumbled over and was running at a tremendous pace down the slope towards Mr. Heelas. [/]
[MrHeelas] “ Lord! ” cried Mr. Heelas, struck with an idea; “ it ’s that Invisible Man brute! [/]
[MrHeelas] It ’s right, after all! ” [/]
[Narrator] With Mr. Heelas to think things like that was to act, and his cook watching him from the top window was amazed to see him come pelting towards the house at a good nine miles an hour. [/]
[Narrator] There was a slamming of doors, a ringing of bells, and the voice of Mr. Heelas bellowing like a bull. [/]
[MrHeelas] “ Shut the doors, shut the windows, shut everything! —the Invisible Man is coming! ” [/]
[Narrator] Instantly the house was full of screams and directions, and scurrying feet. [/]
[Narrator] He ran himself to shut the French windows that opened on the veranda; as he did so Kemp ’s head and shoulders and knee appeared over the edge of the garden fence. [/]
[Narrator] In another moment Kemp had ploughed through the asparagus, and was running across the tennis lawn to the house. [/]
[MrHeelas] “ You ca n’t come in, ” said Mr. Heelas, shutting the bolts. [/]
[MrHeelas] “ I ’m very sorry if he ’s after you, but you ca n’t come in! ” [/]
[Narrator] Kemp appeared with a face of terror close to the glass, rapping and then shaking frantically at the French window. [/]
[Narrator] Then, seeing his efforts were useless, he ran along the veranda, vaulted the end, and went to hammer at the side door. [/]
[Narrator] Then he ran round by the side gate to the front of the house, and so into the hill - road. [/]
[Narrator] And Mr. Heelas staring from his window — a face of horror — had scarcely witnessed Kemp vanish, ere [/]
[Narrator] the asparagus was being trampled this way and that by feet unseen. [/]
[Narrator] At that Mr. Heelas fled precipitately upstairs, and the rest of the chase is beyond his purview. [/]
[Narrator] But as he passed the staircase window, he heard the side gate slam. [/]
[Narrator] Emerging into the hill - road, Kemp naturally took the downward direction, and so it was he came to run in his own person the very race he had watched with such a critical eye from the belvedere study only four days ago. [/]
[Narrator] He ran it well, for a man out of training, and though his face was white and wet, his wits were cool to the last. [/]
[Narrator] He ran with wide strides, and wherever a patch of rough ground intervened, wherever there came a patch of raw flints, or a bit of broken glass shone dazzling, he crossed it and left the bare invisible feet that followed to take what line they would. [/]
[Narrator] For the first time in his life Kemp discovered that the hill - road was indescribably vast and desolate, and that the beginnings of the town far below at the hill foot were strangely remote. [/]
[Narrator] Never had there been a slower or more painful method of progression than running. [/]
[Narrator] All the gaunt villas, sleeping in the afternoon sun, looked locked and barred; no doubt they were locked and barred — by his own orders. [/]
[Narrator] But at any rate they might have kept a lookout for an eventuality like this! [/]
[Narrator] The town was rising up now, the sea had dropped out of sight behind it, and people down below were stirring. [/]
[Narrator] A tram was just arriving at the hill foot. [/]
[Narrator] Beyond that was the police station. [/]
[Narrator] Was that footsteps he heard behind him? [/]
[Narrator] Spurt. [/]
[Narrator] The people below were staring at him, one or two were running, and his breath was beginning to saw in his throat. [/]
[TheJollyCricketers] The tram was quite near now, and the “ Jolly Cricketers ” was noisily barring its doors. [/]
[Narrator] Beyond the tram were posts and heaps of gravel — the drainage works. [/]
[Narrator] He had a transitory idea of jumping into the tram and slamming the doors, and then he resolved to go for the police station. [/]
[TheTramDriver] In another moment he had passed the door of the “ Jolly Cricketers, ” and was in the blistering fag end of the street, with human beings about him. [/]
[Narrator] The tram driver and his helper — arrested by the sight of his furious haste — stood staring with the tram horses unhitched. [/]
[Narrator] Further on the astonished features of navvies appeared above the mounds of gravel. [/]
[Narrator] His pace broke a little, and then he heard the swift pad of his pursuer, and leapt forward again. [/]
[Kemp] “ The Invisible Man! ” [/]
[Narrator] he cried to the navvies, with a vague indicative gesture, and by an inspiration leapt the excavation and placed a burly group between him and the chase. [/]
[Narrator] Then abandoning the idea of the police station he turned into a little side street, rushed by a greengrocer ’s cart, hesitated for the tenth of a second at the door of a sweetstuff shop, and then made for the mouth of an alley that ran back into the main Hill Street again. [/]
[Narrator] Two or three little children were playing here, and shrieked and scattered at his apparition, and forthwith doors and windows opened and excited mothers revealed their hearts. [/]
[Narrator] Out he shot into Hill Street again, three hundred yards from the tram - line end, and immediately he became aware of a tumultuous vociferation and running people. [/]
[Narrator] He glanced up the street towards the hill. [/]
[Narrator] Hardly a dozen yards off ran a huge navvy, cursing in fragments and slashing viciously with a spade, and hard behind him came the tram conductor with his fists clenched. [/]
[Narrator] Up the street others followed these two, striking and shouting. [/]
[Narrator] Down towards the town, men and women were running, and he noticed clearly one man coming out of a shop - door with a stick in his hand. [/]
[SomeOne] “ Spread out! [/]
[SomeOne] Spread out! ” [/]
[Narrator] cried some one. [/]
[Narrator] Kemp suddenly grasped the altered condition of the chase. [/]
[Narrator] He stopped, and looked round, panting. [/]
[Kemp] “ He ’s close here! ” [/]
[Narrator] he cried. [/]
[Kemp] “ Form a line across — ” He was hit hard under the ear, and went reeling, trying to face round towards his unseen antagonist. [/]
[Narrator] He just managed to keep his feet, and he struck a vain counter in the air. [/]
[Narrator] Then he was hit again under the jaw, and sprawled headlong on the ground. [/]
[Narrator] In another moment a knee compressed his diaphragm, and a couple of eager hands gripped his throat, but the grip of one was weaker than the other; he grasped the wrists, heard a cry of pain from his assailant, and then the spade of the navvy came whirling through the air above him, and struck something with a dull thud. [/]
[Narrator] He felt a drop of moisture on his face. [/]
[Narrator] The grip at his throat suddenly relaxed, and with a convulsive effort, Kemp loosed himself, grasped a limp shoulder, and rolled uppermost. [/]
[Narrator] He gripped the unseen elbows near the ground. [/]
[Kemp] “ I ’ve got him! ” screamed Kemp. [/]
[Kemp] “ Help! [/]
[Kemp] Help — hold! [/]
[Kemp] He ’s down! [/]
[Kemp] Hold his feet! ” [/]
[Narrator] In another second there was a simultaneous rush upon the struggle, and a stranger coming into the road suddenly might have thought an exceptionally savage game of Rugby football was in progress. [/]
[Narrator] And there was no shouting after Kemp ’s cry — only a sound of blows and feet and heavy breathing. [/]
[Narrator] Then came a mighty effort, and the Invisible Man threw off a couple of his antagonists and rose to his knees. [/]
[Narrator] Kemp clung to him in front like a hound to a stag, and a dozen hands gripped, clutched, and tore at the Unseen. [/]
[Narrator] The tram conductor suddenly got the neck and shoulders and lugged him back. [/]
[Narrator] Down went the heap of struggling men again and rolled over. [/]
[Narrator] There was, I am afraid, some savage kicking. [/]
[Kemp] Then suddenly a wild scream of “ Mercy! [/]
[Kemp] Mercy! ” [/]
[Narrator] that died down swiftly to a sound like choking. [/]
[Kemp] “ Get back, you fools! ” [/]
[Narrator] cried the muffled voice of Kemp, and there was a vigorous shoving back of stalwart forms. [/]
[Kemp] “ He ’s hurt, I tell you. [/]
[Kemp] Stand back! ” [/]
[Narrator] There was a brief struggle to clear a space, and then the circle of eager faces saw the doctor kneeling, as it seemed, fifteen inches in the air, and holding invisible arms to the ground. [/]
[Narrator] Behind him a constable gripped invisible ankles. [/]
[TheBigNavvy] “ Do n’t you leave go of en, ” cried the big navvy, holding a blood - stained spade; “ he ’s shamming. ” [/]
[TheDoctor] “ He ’s not shamming, ” said the doctor, cautiously raising his knee; “ and I ’ll hold him. ” [/]
[Narrator] His face was bruised and already going red; he spoke thickly because of a bleeding lip. [/]
[Narrator] He released one hand and seemed to be feeling at the face. [/]
[TheDoctor] “ The mouth ’s all wet, ” he said. [/]
[TheDoctor] And then, “ Good God! ” He stood up abruptly and then knelt down on the ground by the side of the thing unseen. [/]
[Narrator] There was a pushing and shuffling, a sound of heavy feet as fresh people turned up to increase the pressure of the crowd. [/]
[Narrator] People now were coming out of the houses. [/]
[Kemp] The doors of the “ Jolly Cricketers ” stood suddenly wide open. [/]
[Narrator] Very little was said. [/]
[Narrator] Kemp felt about, his hand seeming to pass through empty air. [/]
[Kemp] “ He ’s not breathing, ” he said, and then, “ I ca n’t feel his heart. [/]
[Kemp] His side — ugh! ” Suddenly an old woman, peering under the arm of the big navvy, screamed sharply. [/]
[AnOldWomanPeeringUnderTheArmOfTheBigNavvy] “ Looky there! ” [/]
[Narrator] she said, and thrust out a wrinkled finger. [/]
[Narrator] And looking where she pointed, everyone saw, faint and transparent as though it was made of glass, so that veins and arteries and bones and nerves could be distinguished, the outline of a hand, a hand limp and prone. [/]
[Narrator] It grew clouded and opaque even as they stared. [/]
[TheConstable] “ Hullo! ” cried the constable. [/]
[TheConstable] “ Here ’s his feet a - showing! ” [/]
[Narrator] And so, slowly, beginning at his hands and feet and creeping along his limbs to the vital centres of his body, that strange change continued. [/]
[Narrator] It was like the slow spreading of a poison. [/]
[Narrator] First came the little white nerves, a hazy grey sketch of a limb, then the glassy bones and intricate arteries, then the flesh and skin, first a faint fogginess, and then growing rapidly dense and opaque. [/]
[Narrator] Presently they could see his crushed chest and his shoulders, and the dim outline of his drawn and battered features. [/]
[Narrator] When at last the crowd made way for Kemp to stand erect, there lay, naked and pitiful on the ground, the bruised and broken body of a young man about thirty. [/]
[Narrator] His hair and brow were white — not grey with age, but white with the whiteness of albinism — and his eyes were like garnets. [/]
[Narrator] His hands were clenched, his eyes wide open, and his expression was one of anger and dismay. [/]
[AMan] “ Cover his face! ” said a man. [/]
[AMan] “ For Gawd ’s sake, cover that face! ” [/]
[Narrator] and three little children, pushing forward through the crowd, were suddenly twisted round and sent packing off again. [/]
[AMan] Someone brought a sheet from the “ Jolly Cricketers, ” and having covered him, they carried him into that house. [/]
[Narrator] And there it was, on a shabby bed in a tawdry, ill - lighted bedroom, surrounded by a crowd of ignorant and excited people, broken and wounded, betrayed and unpitied, that Griffin, the first of all men to make himself invisible, Griffin, the most gifted physicist the world has ever seen, ended in infinite disaster his strange and terrible career. [/]
[Narrator] THE EPILOGUE So ends the story of the strange and evil experiments of the Invisible Man. [/]
[Narrator] And if you would learn more of him you must go to a little inn near Port Stowe and talk to the landlord. [/]
[Narrator] The sign of the inn is an empty board save for a hat and boots, and the name is the title of this story. [/]
[Narrator] The landlord is a short and corpulent little man with a nose of cylindrical proportions, wiry hair, and a sporadic rosiness of visage. [/]
[Narrator] Drink generously, and he will tell you generously of all the things that happened to him after that time, and of how the lawyers tried to do him out of the treasure found upon him. [/]
[TheLandlord] “ When they found they could n’t prove whose money was which, I ’m blessed, ” he says, “ if they did n’t try to make me out a blooming treasure trove! [/]
[TheLandlord] Do I _ look _ like a Treasure Trove? [/]
[TheLandlord] And then a gentleman gave me a guinea a night to tell the story at the Empire Music ’ All — just to tell ’em in my own words — barring one. ” [/]
[Narrator] And if you want to cut off the flow of his reminiscences abruptly, you can always do so by asking if there were n’t three manuscript books in the story. [/]
[Narrator] He admits there were and proceeds to explain, with asseverations that everybody thinks _ [/]
[Narrator] he _ has ’em! [/]
[Narrator] But bless you! [/]
[Narrator] he has n’t. [/]
[TheLandlord] “ The Invisible Man it was took ’em off to hide ’em when I cut and ran for Port Stowe. [/]
[TheLandlord] It ’s that Mr. Kemp put people on with the idea of _ my _ having ’em. ” [/]
[Narrator] And then he subsides into a pensive state, watches you furtively, bustles nervously with glasses, and presently leaves the bar. [/]
[Narrator] He is a bachelor man — his tastes were ever bachelor, and there are no women folk in the house. [/]
[Narrator] Outwardly he buttons — it is expected of him — but in his more vital privacies, in the matter of braces for example, he still turns to string. [/]
[Narrator] He conducts his house without enterprise, but with eminent decorum. [/]
[Narrator] His movements are slow, and he is a great thinker. [/]
[Narrator] But he has a reputation for wisdom and for a respectable parsimony in the village, and his knowledge of the roads of the South of England would beat Cobbett. [/]
[Narrator] And on Sunday mornings, every Sunday morning, all the year round, while he is closed to the outer world, and every night after ten, he goes into his bar parlour, bearing a glass of gin faintly tinged with water, and having placed this down, he locks the door and examines the blinds, and even looks under the table. [/]
[Narrator] And then, being satisfied of his solitude, he unlocks the cupboard and a box in the cupboard and a drawer in that box, and produces three volumes bound in brown leather, and places them solemnly in the middle of the table. [/]
[Narrator] The covers are weather - worn and tinged with an algal green — for once they sojourned in a ditch and some of the pages have been washed blank by dirty water. [/]
[Narrator] The landlord sits down in an armchair, fills a long clay pipe slowly — gloating over the books the while. [/]
[Narrator] Then he pulls one towards him and opens it, and begins to study it — turning over the leaves backwards and forwards. [/]
[Narrator] His brows are knit and his lips move painfully. [/]
[TheLandlord] “ Hex, little two up in the air, cross and a fiddle - de - dee. Lord! [/]
[TheLandlord] what a one he was for intellect! ” [/]
[Narrator] Presently he relaxes and leans back, and blinks through his smoke across the room at things invisible to other eyes. [/]
[TheLandlord] “ Full of secrets, ” he says. [/]
[TheLandlord] “ Wonderful secrets! ” [/]
[TheLandlord] “ Once I get the haul of them—_Lord _! ” “ I would n’t do what _ [/]
[TheLandlord] he _ did; I ’d just — well! ” [/]
[Narrator] He pulls at his pipe. [/]
[Narrator] So he lapses into a dream, the undying wonderful dream of his life. [/]
[Narrator] And though Kemp has fished unceasingly, no human being save the landlord knows those books are there, with the subtle secret of invisibility and a dozen other strange secrets written therein. [/]
[Narrator] And none other will know of them until he dies. [/]
